      },
      {
        "expected_failure": "The atom at lag 2 equals one and exceeds the shrunk bound; FAIL against expected PASS (killed by vector 1).",
        "id": "mut-stricter-bound",
        "mutation": "The bound constant 6 is replaced by 1.",
        "observed_exit": 1,
        "receipt_sha256": "368340d3f55f0d3b2824db2fc8896e36ea61df58bf389f18e498fb4c2b912467"
      },
      {
        "expected_failure": "Deterministic walk gives atom one at lag 7 for depth 2; FAIL against expected PASS (killed by vector 2).",
        "id": "mut-square-steps",
        "mutation": "Increments are squared, so the walk always moves up by one and loses its randomness.",
        "observed_exit": 1,
        "receipt_sha256": "9e889c9bc1b3dc724d56562114453f66f65205fd1406977b2c3abbd08046c64d"
      }
    ],
    "oracle_code": "import sys, math\nfrom collections import Counter\n\n\ndef verdict(m):\n    T = 3 ** m\n    level = [0]\n    nonleaf = []\n    for _ in range(m):\n        nonleaf += level\n        level = [3 * v + 1 + d for v in level for d in range(3)]\n    leaf_ids = level\n    thirds = sorted(3 * v + 3 for v in nonleaf)\n    pos = {v: i for i, v in enumerate(thirds)}\n    n = len(thirds)\n    seqs = []\n    for mask in range(1 << n):\n        spin = {0: 1}\n        for v in nonleaf:\n            for d in range(3):\n                c = 3 * v + 1 + d\n                if d < 2:\n                    spin[c] = spin[v]\n                else:\n                    spin[c] = 1 if (mask >> pos[c]) & 1 else -1\n        S = [0]\n        for lid in leaf_ids:\n            S.append(S[-1] + spin[lid])\n        seqs.append(S)\n    l2 = math.log(2)\n    l3 = math.log(3)\n    for u in range(T):\n        pid = {}\n        ids = []\n        for S in seqs:\n            t = tuple(S[:u + 1])\n            if t not in pid:\n                pid[t] = len(pid)\n            ids.append(pid[t])\n        pre = Counter(ids)\n        for k in range(1, T - u + 1):\n            joint = Counter()\n            for S, i in zip(seqs, ids):\n                joint[i * 128 + S[u + k] + 64] += 1\n            for key, cnt in joint.items():\n                i = key // 128\n                p = cnt / pre[i]\n                if math.log(p) * l3 > math.log(6.0 / k) * l2 + 1e-12:\n                    return \"FAIL\"\n    return \"PASS\"\n\n\ndef main():\n    inp, out, expected = sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2], sys.argv[3]\n    m = int(open(inp).read().split()[0])\n    got = open(out).read().strip()\n    mine = verdict(m)\n    if got == expected.strip() and mine == expected.strip():\n        sys.exit(0)\n    sys.exit(1)\n\n\nmain()\n",
    "output_contract": "One line: PASS if all exact conditional atoms satisfy atom <= (6/k)^sigma with sigma=log2/log3, FAIL otherwise.",
    "reason": "Lemma 33 gives an explicit finite construction (ternary tree with inherited and fresh fair spins); for small depth the exact conditional atoms can be enumerated and compared with the stated bound. The headline asymptotic theorem is not finite and is not covered.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: one integer m (tree depth) on stdin\n# PRECONDITIONS: 0 <= m <= 3 so that all fresh-spin assignments can be enumerated\n# OUTPUTS: PASS if every exact conditional atom of the leaf-sum process obeys the stated power bound for all u,k with u+k<=3^m, else FAIL\n# TIME: O(2^n * T^2) with T=3^m and n=(T-1)/2\n# SPACE: O(2^n * T)\n# ARITHMETIC: exact integer counts; the bound uses double-precision powers with 1e-12 slack\n# FAILURE: m outside the range raises ValueError (nonzero exit)\nimport sys, math\nfrom itertools import product\n\nSIGMA = math.log(2) / math.log(3)\n\n\ndef leaves(depth, spin, it):\n    if depth == 0:\n        return [spin]\n    a = leaves(depth - 1, spin, it)\n    b = leaves(depth - 1, spin, it)\n    f = next(it)\n    c = leaves(depth - 1, f, it)\n    return a + b + c\n\n\nm = int(sys.stdin.read().split()[0])\nif m < 0 or m > 3:\n    raise ValueError(\"depth out of range\")\nT = 3 ** m\nn = (T - 1) // 2\nseqs = []\nfor bits in product((-1, 1), repeat=n):\n    it = iter(bits)\n    sp = leaves(m, 1, it)\n    S = [0]\n    for x in sp:\n        S.append(S[-1] + x)\n    seqs.append(S)\nok = True\nfor u in range(T):\n    groups = {}\n    for S in seqs:\n        groups.setdefault(tuple(S[:u + 1]), []).append(S)\n    for g in groups.values():\n        for k in range(1, T - u + 1):\n            c = {}\n            for S in g:\n                v = S[u + k]\n                c[v] = c.get(v, 0) + 1\n            p = max(c.values()) / len(g)\n            if p > (6.0 / k) ** SIGMA + 1e-12:\n                ok = False\nprint(\"PASS\" if ok else \"FAIL\")\n",
    "test_vectors": [

      },

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorem is an existence statement for a threshold density with no explicit value and no finite procedure to implement, so no library can be derived from it. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result concerns a zero-temperature quantum ground state in normalised units; it supplies no real-time dynamics or physics-engine rule that a game or interactive simulation could use. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No reusable checker, solver or analysis utility follows from the proofs; the statements are asymptotic estimates uniform over ground states, not tool specifications. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Brownian-bridge couplings and random routes appear only as proof devices for bounding likelihoods; they are not offered as algorithms with guarantees. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper introduces spectral and functional-analytic objects (resolvents, projections), with no data layout or access-cost question. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no computational kernel, arithmetic scheme or dataflow in the paper; the torus setting is purely mathematical. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns hardness assumptions, adversaries or protocols; it is a many-body quantum depletion theorem. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The test functions are bounded continuous functions used for weak convergence of a measure; there is no learning problem, loss or generalisation claim. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Energy bounds are variational lower and upper estimates for a ground state, not an optimisation instance with a solver or approximation ratio. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Thermodynamic sequences of particle number and volume are a limiting regime of one quantum system; there are no nodes, messages or fault models. |
| other (Benchmarking of many-body quantum simulation) | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | The limiting momentum measure and its total mass give a reference value against which a numerical ground-state method for dilute hard spheres could be compared in the stated regime. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-iterated-limit-order** (hypothesis): The conclusion is for a fixed small density: the thermodynamic limit is taken first at that density, and only afterwards is the dilute regime used. It gives no statement for a single finite system, and no uniformity in the density. Blocked use: ['Quoting the depletion asymptotic as an error bound for a finite particle number or finite box', 'Exchanging the order of the thermodynamic and dilute limits']
- **limit-density-threshold-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): The density threshold depends on the exclusion distance, the test function and the tolerance, and only its existence is asserted. No explicit value or formula is given. Blocked use: ['Using the theorem to certify a specific physical gas as being in the valid regime', 'Hard-coding a numerical threshold in a library or benchmark']
- **limit-sequence-hypothesis** (hypothesis): The statement is made along sequences with particle number and torus side tending to infinity whose density converges to the fixed density. A convergence rate of that density is not provided, and the limsup form controls accumulation values only. Blocked use: ['Reading the theorem as the existence of a unique limit at fixed positive gas parameter', 'Using it for sequences whose density does not converge']
- **limit-uniform-over-ground-states** (formal_scope): The depletion asymptotic is asserted uniformly over ground states of the zero-temperature hard-sphere Hamiltonian, mixed states supported in the ground eigenspace included. Excited or thermal states are outside the statement. Blocked use: ['Applying the depletion coefficient to thermal states', 'Applying the coefficient to states not supported on the lowest eigenspace']
- **limit-hard-core-model** (formal_scope): The analysis is for pure hard-core exclusion with the exclusion distance equal to the scattering length, in normalised units. Soft, attractive or long-range interactions are not covered. Blocked use: ['Transferring the profile and total mass to soft or attractive potentials', 'Reading the constants in dimensional units without rescaling']
- **limit-box-density-range** (edge_case): The box lower bound is stated only for particle numbers up to a fixed multiple of the mean number in a box. Sectors with more particles need a separate batching argument and are not covered by the stated inequality. Blocked use: ['Applying the rough box inequality to arbitrarily overcrowded boxes']
- **limit-low-excitation-sector** (hypothesis): The sharp energy bound with a retained quasiparticle term holds only on vectors supported where the number of low-frequency excitations is capped. The vector must lie in the form domain of the replacement Hamiltonian and in the middle particle-number range. Blocked use: ['Applying the retained-quasiparticle inequality to unlocalised vectors', 'Applying it to vectors outside the middle particle-number sector']
- **limit-routes-parameter-regime** (hypothesis): The route construction needs a large enough scale ratio, a torus large compared with that scale, and a small bad-site probability constant. Outside this regime the encounter bounds are not claimed. Blocked use: ['Using the exponential-moment and rare-encounter bounds on small tori', 'Using them when bad sites are not sparse in the joint sense']
- **limit-environment-averaged-routes** (approximation): The encounter estimates are averages over the random environment. They are not pointwise statements for each fixed configuration of bad sites. Blocked use: ['Using the exponential moment bound for one fixed defect configuration', 'Treating the rare-encounter estimate as a worst-case guarantee']
- **limit-collision-constant-ineffective** (approximation): The collision and fourth-moment savings are written with unspecified constants depending on auxiliary parameters, and are valid only for sufficiently large density. They give power savings, not numerical error bars. Blocked use: ['Quoting a numerical error for a given density from the collision bound', 'Using the saving power at moderate densities']
- **limit-low-count-parameters** (hypothesis): The low-count suppression requires a sufficiently large scaled constant and then a sufficiently small count threshold, both unspecified. Blocked use: ['Using the suppression bound with arbitrary constants chosen by the user']
- **limit-unpredictable-walk-lag** (edge_case): The conditional atom decay holds only for positive lags within the finite horizon, for the specific tree construction. Lag zero has atom one and no decay. Blocked use: ['Quoting the decay at lag zero or past the horizon', 'Applying it to other walks without re-deriving']
- **limit-convergence-rate-absent** (unknown): No rate for the convergence of the density along the sequence or for the inner limit is given. The dilute remainder is only o-notation. Blocked use: ['Deriving finite-size corrections from the theorem']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the full scaled Bogoliubov momentum distribution for the depleted particles in the three-dimensional hard-sphere Bose gas at zero temperature. The thermodynamic limit is taken at each fixed density before the dilute limit, uniformly over all pure and mixed ground states. All thermodynamic accumulation values have the same dilute asymptotic: the limiting nonzero-momentum occupation measure has total mass $`8/(3\sqrt\pi)`$, giving the depletion fraction $`\frac{8}{3\sqrt\pi}\sqrt{\rho a^3}+o(\sqrt{\rho a^3})`$ for density <i>ρ</i> and hard-sphere exclusion distance <i>a</i>.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Interactions move particles out of the condensate even in a Bose gas at zero temperature. For a dilute three-dimensional gas, Bogoliubov theory predicts both the number of these depleted particles and their distribution in momentum. Establishing these predictions for an exact ground state requires information at arbitrarily long wavelengths: their energy cost tends to zero as the volume grows. This paper determines the leading momentum distribution for hard spheres, with the ordinary thermodynamic limit taken before the dilute limit.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The model and the result

Fix $a>0$ and write $\Lambda_L=(\mathbb R/L\mathbb Z)^3$ for the flat torus of side $L>2a$, with torus distance $d_L$. The allowed configuration space is $$\Omega_{N,L,a}=\{(x_1,\ldots,x_N)\in\Lambda_L^N:
                    d_L(x_i,x_j)>a\text{ for }i<j\}.$$ The hard-sphere Hamiltonian $H^{\mathrm{hc}}_{N,L,a}$ is the operator associated with the quadratic form $$q[\Psi]=\sum_{i=1}^N\int_{\Omega_{N,L,a}}|\nabla_i\Psi|^2,
 \qquad
 \mathcal D(q)=H_0^1(\Omega_{N,L,a})\cap L^2_{\mathrm{sym}}(\Omega_{N,L,a}).$$ Here $H_0^1$ is the $H^1$ closure of smooth functions compactly supported in the allowed configuration set; the ambient torus is already periodic. We use units $\hbar^2/(2m)=1$. The parameter $a$ is both the exclusion distance between centers and the scattering length: the exterior zero-energy scattering solution is $1-a/|x|$. The form domain is nontrivial along every sequence with $N/L^3\to\rho<1/(8a^3)$ and $N,L\to\infty$: a cubic grid of spacing at least $2a$ eventually provides $N$ sites, and small neighborhoods of these sites give an allowed open configuration set. Zero extension and compactness on the full torus then give compact resolvent.

<a id="intro:profile"></a>
Let $P_{N,L,a}$ be the projection onto the lowest bosonic eigenspace, and let $\mathcal G_{N,L,a}$ consist of the positive trace-one operators $\Gamma$ satisfying $\Gamma=P_{N,L,a}\Gamma P_{N,L,a}$. After extension by zero to the full periodic configuration space, put $$\gamma^{(1)}_\Gamma=N\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{2,\ldots,N}\Gamma,
 \qquad u_{k,L}(x)=L^{-3/2}e^{ik\cdot x}\quad
                    (k\in(2\pi/L)\mathbb Z^3),$$ and define $$n_{\Gamma,L}(k)=\langle u_{k,L},\gamma^{(1)}_\Gamma u_{k,L}\rangle,
 \qquad
 B_{N,L,a}(\Gamma)=\frac{\langle u_{0,L},\gamma^{(1)}_\Gamma u_{0,L}\rangle}{N}.$$ Thus $B_{N,L,a}$ is the occupation fraction of the specified constant orbital, and $1-B_{N,L,a}$ is its depletion. The total occupation is $\sum_k n_{\Gamma,L}(k)=N$. For $t\in\mathbb R^3\setminus\{0\}$, set $$\label{intro:profile}
 g(t)=\frac{|t|^2+1}{2\sqrt{|t|^4+2|t|^2}}-\frac12,
 \qquad
 \nu_{\mathrm{Bog}}(\mathrm dt)
       =\frac{(8\pi)^{3/2}}{(2\pi)^3}g(t)\,\mathrm dt.$$ This is a finite positive measure: $g(t)=O(|t|^{-1})$ near zero and $g(t)=O(|t|^{-4})$ at infinity. Its total mass is $8/(3\sqrt\pi)$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="intro:main-limit"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *For every $a>0$, every bounded continuous real function $f$ on $\mathbb R^3$, and every $\varepsilon>0$, there is $\rho_0(a,f,\varepsilon)>0$ with the following property. Fix $0<\rho<\rho_0$ and put $\eta=\rho a^3$. For every sequence $N_j,L_j\longrightarrow\infty$ with $N_j/L_j^3\longrightarrow\rho$, $$\label{intro:main-limit}
 \limsup_{j\to\infty}\ \sup_{\Gamma\in\mathcal G_{N_j,L_j,a}}
 \left|
 \frac{1}{N_j\sqrt\eta}
 \sum_{\substack{k\in(2\pi/L_j)\mathbb Z^3\\k\ne0}}
 f\!\left(\frac{k}{\sqrt{8\pi\rho a}}\right)n_{\Gamma,L_j}(k)
       -\int_{\mathbb R^3}f\,\mathrm d\nu_{\mathrm{Bog}}
 \right|\le\varepsilon.$$*

</div>

The density is fixed throughout each thermodynamic limit. In particular, the theorem controls every thermodynamic accumulation value of the occupation, without requiring a unique occupation limit at fixed positive $\eta$. Its uniformity includes all normalized, possibly complex ground vectors and the normalized projection onto the entire ground eigenspace.

<a id="stmt-cor-depletion"></a>
<div id="cor:depletion" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *In the same iterated limit, uniformly over all ground states, $$1-B_{N,L,a}(\Gamma)
   =\frac8{3\sqrt\pi}\sqrt{\rho a^3}+o(\sqrt{\rho a^3}).$$*

</div>

This follows by taking $f=1$. The theorem also determines how the depleted particles are distributed on the momentum scale $\sqrt{8\pi\rho a}$, without assuming translation invariance of an individual ground state.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Historical context and the sources of the method

Bogoliubov’s description of a weakly interacting Bose gas replaces its low-energy excitations by quasiparticles obtained from a linear canonical transformation <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Bogoliubov](#ref-Bogoliubov)]. The quasiparticle vacuum already has particles in nonzero momentum modes. Lee, Huang, and Yang developed the hard-sphere pseudopotential calculation far enough to determine both the second term of the energy and the leading depletion <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[LHY](#ref-LHY)]. Thus the predicted momentum profile and the coefficient $8/(3\sqrt\pi)$ describe the many-body state, as well as the fluctuations responsible for the energy correction. The distinction matters mathematically: matching the energy does not by itself control the occupation of modes whose energy tends to zero with the inverse size of the system.

The rigorous energy theory began with Dyson’s bounds for hard spheres <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Dyson](#ref-Dyson)]; Lieb and Yngvason later obtained the matching leading lower bound <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[LY](#ref-LY)]. At the Lee–Huang–Yang order, Yau and Yin proved the upper bound for smooth repulsive short-range potentials <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[YY](#ref-YY)]. Basti, Cenatiempo, and Schlein subsequently gave a proof for nonnegative radial compactly supported $L^3$ potentials with an improved error <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[BCS2021](#ref-BCS2021)]. Fournais and Solovej established the lower bound, first for integrable interactions and then for a class including hard cores <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[FS2020](#ref-FS2020), [FS2023](#ref-FS2023)]. For hard spheres, Basti, Cenatiempo, Giuliani, Olgiati, Pasqualetti, and Schlein bounded the correction by a constant times the Lee–Huang–Yang scale <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BCGOPS](#ref-BCGOPS)]; Basti, Brooks, Cenatiempo, Olgiati, and Schlein obtained the sharp coefficient <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BBCOS](#ref-BBCOS)]. Their subsequent work extends this upper-bound method to measurable even nonnegative compactly supported potentials, including hard cores <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BBCOSgeneral](#ref-BBCOSgeneral)]. The hard-sphere upper bound supplies the energy comparison used here.

Rigorous information about the state has developed in several different scaling limits. Lieb and Seiringer proved complete condensation for trapped gases in the Gross–Pitaevskii limit, allowing hard cores; their result includes trace-norm convergence of the normalized one-particle density matrix and the corresponding leading momentum-density limit <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[LS2002](#ref-LS2002)]. For a periodic unit box with scattering length of order $N^{-1}$, Boccato, Brennecke, Cenatiempo, and Schlein determined the next-order depletion for nonnegative radial compactly supported $L^3(\mathbb R^3)$ potentials <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BBCS](#ref-BBCS), Equation (1.14) and Appendix A]. The associated discrete momentum occupations are expressed as a trace-norm expansion of the one-particle density matrix by Brennecke, Lee, and Nam, who also establish its positive-temperature analogue <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BLN](#ref-BLN), Equations (6)–(8) and Theorem 1.1]. The second-order density-matrix expansion resolves the individual discrete occupations as well as their sum. Its relation between volume and scattering length differs from the thermodynamic limit at fixed density considered here.

Condensation on boxes growing with the dilute parameter was proved by Fournais <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Fournais2021](#ref-Fournais2021)], and the propagation of such estimates to larger scales has been developed further by Junge <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Junge2026](#ref-Junge2026)]. These results keep the box size tied to the dilution. In the ordinary thermodynamic limit, the one-particle gap vanishes at each fixed positive density, so an additional estimate is needed for variation across the whole torus. A different comparison is provided by the “simple equation” approximation to the Bose gas: Carlen, Jauslin, and Lieb derived its leading depletion coefficient <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CJL](#ref-CJL), Theorem 1.6], and Jauslin derived its Bogoliubov momentum profile on the inverse healing-length scale <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Jauslin2024](#ref-Jauslin2024), Theorem 3]. Those are results for an approximate correlation equation. The present occupation measures are those of the exact many-body hard-sphere ground states, and their convergence is weak convergence of finite measures on the rescaled momentum space.

Our local energy analysis builds on the lower-bound methods of Fournais and Solovej <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[FS2020](#ref-FS2020), [FS2023](#ref-FS2023)] and the Neumann-box localization of Haberberger, Hainzl, Nam, Seiringer, and Triay <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[HHNST](#ref-HHNST)]. Fournais, Junge, Girardot, Morin, Olivieri, and Triay combined these approaches in their treatment of strong interactions at low temperature <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[FJGMOT](#ref-FJGMOT)]. We use intermediate operator estimates from that work, retaining a positive term that measures Bogoliubov excitations, before taking an observable expectation. This is a use of their operator analysis beyond its scalar free-energy conclusion. The excitation-number localization is related to the large-matrix localization of Lieb and Solovej <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[LS2001](#ref-LS2001), Appendix A], and the coherent-state treatment of the condensate mode uses the $c$-number substitution of Lieb, Seiringer, and Yngvason <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[LSY2005](#ref-LSY2005)].

The long-distance comparison uses a stationary ground-state path representation, a form of Doob’s $h$-transform <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Doob](#ref-Doob)]. The spatial count estimates and exact conditional killed-Brownian-path laws used here are supplied by the companion paper <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[H](#ref-H)]. The companion establishes a positive constant-orbital occupation uniformly over the hard-sphere ground space. Here the comparison of particle-deletion measures must be accurate below the depletion scale. Its additional quantitative ingredients are a local fourth-moment comparison and a distant second-moment comparison. These compare the deletion measures obtained by averaging the deleted position over different cells.

The route construction adapts the unpredictable-path method of Benjamini, Pemantle, and Peres, who constructed paths in three dimensions with exponential intersection tails <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[BPP](#ref-BPP)]. Our comparison also requires encounters to become rare after the segments near the common endpoint are removed, and it must accommodate finite defective regions. Detours use Kesten’s exterior-boundary connectivity theorem in the formulation of Timár <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Timar](#ref-Timar), Theorem 4]. These geometric inputs are combined with two-copy likelihood estimates that retain the exact conditional normalization; the intersection theorem alone does not give the deletion-measure comparison.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### From local excitations to the momentum profile

<a id="intro:split"></a>
The proof separates excitations inside moderately large boxes from variation between those boxes. For a partition into cubes of side $\ell$, let $P_\ell$ be the one-particle orthogonal projection onto functions constant on each cube, and let $P_0$ be projection onto the single global constant. Since $P_0\le P_\ell$, every normalized ground vector satisfies the exact identity $$\label{intro:split}
 1-B_{N,L,a}(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|)
 =\langle\Psi,(1-P_\ell)_1\Psi\rangle
   +\|(P_\ell-P_0)_1\Psi\|^2.$$ The subscript $1$ means that the projection acts on the first particle. Bosonic symmetry gives the same identity with the average over all particles.

The first term can be analyzed through the energy, provided the lower bound retains a positive operator measuring Bogoliubov excitations. The energy boxes contain several of the projection cells of side $\ell$; those cells will in turn be subdivided into small cells for the spatial comparison. An energy expansion alone does not identify this observable. We use the hard-sphere upper bound of Basti, Brooks, Cenatiempo, Olgiati, and Schlein <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[BBCOS](#ref-BBCOS)], together with intermediate operator estimates of Fournais, Junge, Girardot, Morin, Olivieri, and Triay <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[FJGMOT](#ref-FJGMOT)]. Their positive excitation terms give quantitative closeness to the local quadratic vacuum. We extract from them a uniform estimate for one-body operators supported on the local excitations, including operators whose matrices are complex and not diagonal in momentum. This stronger observable estimate will allow us to test translations after the long-wavelength contribution has been controlled.

The second term concerns wavelengths larger than $\ell$. To control it, we first take a normalized nonnegative ground vector $\Phi$. For a cube $v$ in a torus of volume $V$, define a measure on the remaining $N-1$ coordinates $Y'$ by the density <a id="intro:palm"></a>$$\label{intro:palm}
 s_v(Y')^2=\frac{V}{|v|}\int_v\Phi(y,Y')^2\,\mathrm dy.$$ This is a finite smoothed particle-deletion measure: the deleted particle is selected with weight proportional to its presence in $v$, and its position is integrated out. Its mass is the expected particle count in $v$ divided by $(N/V)|v|$; this mass averages to one over equal-volume cells. Comparing the square roots $s_v$ for distant cubes gives control of spatial variation that the energy cannot supply.

The comparison transports the deletion along a chain of particles. Two copies of the configuration are coupled so that deleting the first particle in one copy and the last in the other leaves identical remaining configurations. Short Brownian bridges implement the rearrangement while preserving the exact hard-sphere constraints. The normalization of the conditional bridge law cancels in the likelihood ratio. Near either end of a long chain, the paths in one copy are kept unchanged. These unchanged segments remove endpoint interactions from the errors measured by a second moment. Random routes with sparse intersections then make that error smaller than the depletion scale.

Three estimates connect these deletion measures to the projection. A fourth-moment comparison between neighboring small-cell root mean squares shows that their arithmetic mean is close to their root mean square over a cell of side $\ell$. The occupation estimate on those same small cells compares their root mean squares with arithmetic averages of $\Phi$. A second-moment comparison between distant cells of side $\ell$ makes their root mean squares nearly constant. Together these estimates bound the last term of <a href="#intro:split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:split">[intro:split]</a> by $o(\sqrt{\rho a^3})$. A decomposition into real and imaginary positive parts extends that bound to every ground vector.

It remains to identify the momentum distribution of these local excitations. Translation by a vector $h$ multiplies a plane wave of momentum $k$ by $e^{ik\cdot h}$, so expectations of translations are Fourier transforms of the occupation measure. We restrict a translation to large boxes and average over shifts of the box partition. Only a small fraction of the shifted partitions separate a point from its translate. Sandwiching the translation between projections onto local excitations makes this error proportional to the depleted occupation. The local one-body estimate then gives the Bogoliubov Fourier transform and convergence on compactly supported tests. Together with total depletion, this supplies tightness and convergence against every bounded continuous function. All estimates are uniform in pure ground vectors, and convexity gives the mixed-state assertion.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Organization.

Section <a href="#sec:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:setup">[sec:setup]</a> sets the scaling and states the probabilistic inputs. Section <a href="#sec:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:energy">[sec:energy]</a> retains the positive excitation operators in the local energy bound, and Section <a href="#en:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="en:consequences">4</a> extracts the count and one-body estimates. Sections <a href="#sec:local-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local-paths">[sec:local-paths]</a> and <a href="#sec:local-palm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local-palm">[sec:local-palm]</a> construct the short-path comparison and prove its fourth-moment bound. Sections <a href="#sec:routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:routes">[sec:routes]</a> and <a href="#sec:long-palm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:long-palm">[sec:long-palm]</a> construct the long routes and compare distant deletion measures. Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">[sec:assembly]</a> combines the estimates to control the long wavelengths. Section <a href="#sec:momentum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:momentum">[sec:momentum]</a> uses averaged translations to prove Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="set:section"></a>
### Scaled variables and ground-state estimates

<span id="sec:setup" label="sec:setup"></span>

We work in units in which the particle density is large and the product of density and scattering length stays in a fixed positive interval. The momentum scale in the theorem is then of order one. The proof must identify the distribution of excitations on that scale and control the particles at longer wavelengths. We introduce the spatial projections that separate these tasks, specify the order of limits, and state the ground-state estimates from <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[H](#ref-H)] used in the spatial comparison.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The form domain and the constant orbital

For an integer $K\ge100$, put $\Lambda_K=(\mathbb R/K\mathbb Z)^3$ and $V=K^3$. The exclusion distance in these units is denoted by $r$. We use the allowed configuration set and Dirichlet form from the introduction, with $(L,a)$ replaced by $(K,r)$, and write $E_n$ for the lowest $n$-particle energy on this same torus with this same exclusion distance. Set $E_0=0$. When evaluating a nonsymmetric function, $q$ denotes the same Dirichlet form without the bosonic restriction. All functions are extended by zero to the full configuration torus. In particular, a normalized bosonic ground vector $\Phi$ has $\|\Phi\|_2=1$ on $\Lambda_K^N$.

<a id="set:scales"></a>
The scaled parameters are $$\label{set:scales}
 D=\frac NV,\qquad \alpha=Dr,\qquad
 \alpha\in I_\alpha:=[\alpha_0/2,2\alpha_0],\qquad
 Dr^3=\frac{\alpha^3}{D^2}.$$ Here $\alpha_0$ is a sufficiently large absolute constant, fixed as in the spatial estimates of <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[H](#ref-H)]. We subsequently increase the lower threshold on $D$ as needed. Constants may depend on the fixed interval $I_\alpha$ and on auxiliary parameters already chosen; they are independent of $K,N$ and the ground vector.

Write $Y=(x_2,\ldots,x_N)$, let $u_0=V^{-1/2}$ on $\Lambda_K$, and let $P_0=|u_0\rangle\langle u_0|$. For a pure state we abbreviate the occupation as <a id="set:occupation"></a>$$\label{set:occupation}
 B(\Phi)=\|(P_0)_1\Phi\|_2^2
   =\frac1V\int_{\Lambda_K^{N-1}}
       \left|\int_{\Lambda_K}\Phi(y,Y)\,\mathrm dy\right|^2\mathrm dY.$$ The subscript denotes the coordinate on which a one-particle operator acts. When $\Phi$ is nonnegative, we also use its configuration law <a id="set:mu"></a>$$\label{set:mu}
 \mu(\mathrm dX)=\Phi(X)^2\,\mathrm dX.$$ For general complex vectors, configuration expectations use $|\Phi|^2\,\mathrm dX$; any restriction to nonnegative vectors will be stated.

<a id="stmt-set-ground-space"></a>
<div id="set:ground-space" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Ground spaces and allowed slices). *For every nonempty allowed configuration set with nontrivial form domain, the full labelled and bosonic Dirichlet operators have compact resolvent and the same lowest energy. A bosonic ground vector is therefore also a ground vector of the full labelled operator. Its modulus is a nonnegative bosonic ground vector. A form-domain function restricts, for almost every choice of some fixed coordinates, to the Dirichlet form domain of the open allowed set in the remaining coordinates. Zero extension of such a slice remains admissible if obstacles contributed by the fixed coordinates are removed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-set-ground-space"><summary>Proof of stmt-set-ground-space</summary>

*Proof.* Zero extension sends $H_0^1$ of the allowed set into $H^1$ of the compact configuration torus. The compact Sobolev embedding proves compactness of the form embedding, and hence compact resolvent. For a labelled form-domain function $f$, its root-mean-square symmetrization $$F(X)=\left(\frac1{n!}\sum_{\pi\in S_n}|f(\pi X)|^2\right)^{1/2}$$ has the same norm and no larger Dirichlet energy, by the gradient inequality for a vector norm. To justify the Dirichlet condition, first apply this construction to smooth compactly supported approximants. Their symmetrizations have compact support in the allowed set, admit interior smooth approximation, and are bounded in $H^1$. Weak closedness of $H_0^1$ gives the assertion for $f$. The full and bosonic variational infima consequently agree. The modulus contraction gives the statement about $|\Phi|$ by the same variational principle.

For slicing, take smooth compactly supported approximants converging in the form norm. Fubini’s theorem gives a subsequence converging in the Sobolev norm of the unfixed variables for almost every fixed tuple. Each approximant has compact support in its open allowed slice, proving the stated Dirichlet membership. Removing fixed obstacles enlarges that open set, so zero extension preserves membership. This argument does not require regularity or connectedness of its boundary. These are the ground-space and slicing arguments of <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[H](#ref-H), Lemmas 2.1 and 2.2]. ◻

</details>

For later use, a real bounded Lipschitz multiplier $u$ satisfies <a id="set:multiplier"></a>$$\label{set:multiplier}
 q[u\Phi]-E_N\|u\Phi\|_2^2
       =\int |\Phi|^2|\nabla u|^2$$ for every ground vector $\Phi$. Indeed test its full weak eigenfunction equation with $u^2\Phi$, take real parts, and expand the two gradients. Multiplication by $u$ preserves the form domain.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Partitions and the two limiting operations

<a id="set:exponents"></a>
Choose small positive exponents $u,w$ with $$\label{set:exponents}
 0<w<u/10000.$$ The energy estimates will impose a further absolute upper bound on $u$. We use integers $R,B$ such that $B$ is a multiple of $R$ and <a id="set:lengths"></a>$$\label{set:lengths}
 R\asymp D^w,\qquad B\asymp D^u,
 \qquad d=1/j\quad(j\in\mathbb N).$$ The fixed factors in these lengths will be specified below to match the length convention in the local energy estimates. We take $K$ to be a multiple of $B$. This entails no restriction on the physical thermodynamic sequences, as the final change of units will show. The partitions into $B$-cells, $R$-cells, unit cells, and $d$-cells are nested. Denote the partition into cells of side $l$ by $\mathcal C_l$. One choice is to place all grid faces at $-1/2+l\mathbb Z$ in each coordinate. Unit cells are then $B_v=v+[-1/2,1/2)^3$, indexed by $\mathcal L=(\mathbb Z/K\mathbb Z)^3$.

For $l\in\{B,R,1,d\}$ define the orthogonal projection <a id="set:cell-projection"></a>$$\label{set:cell-projection}
 (P_l f)(y)=\sum_{A\in\mathcal C_l}\mathbf 1_A(y)
                   \frac1{|A|}\int_A f(z)\,\mathrm dz,
 \qquad W_l=1-P_l.$$ Thus $\sum_i(W_l)_i$ counts particles outside the space of functions constant on each $l$-cell. The notation $\mathrm d\Gamma(A)=\sum_i A_i$ will also be used for a one-particle operator $A$. For a cell $A$ write $n_A(X)=\sum_i\mathbf 1_A(x_i)$. An average over cells always means the uniform average $|\mathcal C_l|^{-1}\sum_{A\in\mathcal C_l}$.

<a id="set:scale-factors"></a>
<a id="set:nested-lengths"></a>
We use the following convention for all asymptotic estimates. In the inner, thermodynamic limit, $K\to\infty$ and the actual scaled parameters converge to fixed values $D_*\ge D_0$ and $\alpha_*\in I_\alpha$. The integers $B,R,j$ are fixed during this limit. For $\theta\in\{u,w\}$ define the fixed factor $$\label{set:scale-factors}
 b_\theta=\alpha_0^{-(1+3\theta)/2},$$ and choose $$\label{set:nested-lengths}
 R=\lceil b_wD_*^w\rceil,
 \qquad B=R\left\lceil\frac{b_uD_*^u}{R}\right\rceil.$$ As $D_*\to\infty$, these choices satisfy $R/(b_wD_*^w)\to1$ and $B/(b_uD_*^u)\to1$; the latter uses $w<u$. Their comparability with the powers of the actual $D$ holds eventually along each inner sequence. The factors in <a href="#set:scale-factors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="set:scale-factors">[set:scale-factors]</a> ensure that the auxiliary density of the local energy theorem stays within a fixed factor of $D$, uniformly in $\alpha\in I_\alpha$. The exact calculation is given in Section <a href="#en:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="en:section">3</a>.

A displayed dilute error $o(1)$ denotes a bound whose thermodynamic limsup tends to zero as $D_*\to\infty$, uniformly in the ground vector and in $\alpha_*\in I_\alpha$. If auxiliary parameters such as $j$ are fixed, the error may depend on them. We write $o(D^{-1})$ for $D^{-1}o(1)$ in this convention. An explicitly written $o_K(1)$ vanishes along the inner limit, uniformly in the ground vector; its volume threshold may depend on $D_*,\alpha_*$ and the fixed auxiliary parameters. Estimates with powers of $D$ have this convention whenever they use a thermodynamic energy upper bound. At fixed $D_*$, any $o_K(1)$ can be made smaller than a prescribed positive multiple of $D_*^{-\beta}$ by increasing the volume threshold. After enlarging the constant, a bound $CD^{-\beta}$ can therefore be written without an additional volume remainder when it is understood to hold beyond that threshold. Estimates stated for every large finite volume do not need this convention. In particular, <a id="set:limit-order"></a>$$\label{set:limit-order}
 o(D^{-1})+o_K(1)
 \quad\hbox{implies}\quad
 \lim_{D_*\to\infty}D_*
       \limsup_{K\to\infty}\bigl(o(D^{-1})+o_K(1)\bigr)=0.$$ No bound on the speed of the inner convergence is required. The microcell parameter $j$ will ultimately be chosen to make an error $\epsilon_j$ small. It is chosen before the dilute threshold and the volume threshold; the exponents $u,w$ and the length $R$ need not change when $j$ is increased.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Spatial inputs from the condensation theorem

The companion condensation theorem <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[H](#ref-H)] supplies the removal-energy and spatial-count estimates below. They provide particles with which to construct the path comparisons and bound the cost of discarding crowded or poorly populated regions. The count estimates concern the law of a nonnegative ground function; the energy argument and the final momentum statement also apply to complex ground vectors.

<a id="stmt-set-removal"></a>
<div id="set:removal" class="proposition">

**Proposition 4** (Removal energies). *For all sufficiently large $D$, uniformly in <a href="#set:scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="set:scales">[set:scales]</a>, <a id="set:removal-bound"></a>$$\label{set:removal-bound}
 E_N-E_{N-m}\le C\alpha m\qquad(0\le m\le N).$$ At fixed $K,r$, the ratio $E_n/n$ is nondecreasing among positive particle numbers having nonempty form domain.*

</div>

This is <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[H](#ref-H), Corollary 3.4 and Equation (3.9)]. Its insertion argument normalizes the new orbital separately for each old configuration; the old kinetic energy therefore incurs no multiplicative loss. Together with the full ground equation, this estimate is the input to deletion of selected path interactions.

<a id="stmt-set-low-counts"></a>
<div id="set:low-counts" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5** (Joint low-count bound). *One can fix $\alpha_0$ sufficiently large and then $\ell_*>0$ sufficiently small so that every normalized nonnegative bosonic ground function satisfies, for every $A\subset\mathcal L$, <a id="set:low-count-bound"></a>$$\label{set:low-count-bound}
 \mu\{n_{B_v}<\ell_*D\text{ for every }v\in A\}
           \le (C/D^2)^{|A|}.$$ The estimate holds for all sufficiently large $D$, uniformly in $K$ and the ground function.*

</div>

This is <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[H](#ref-H), Proposition 3.5 and Equation (3.10)]. The constants in this proposition are fixed before the subsequent path cutoffs are chosen.

<a id="stmt-set-count-mgf"></a>
<div id="set:count-mgf" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6** (Count exponential moments). *For $A\subset\mathcal L$ set $$f_A(X)=\sum_{i=1}^N e^{-d_K(x_i,A)},
 \qquad f_{\varnothing}=0,$$ where the distance is to the set of site centers. Every normalized nonnegative bosonic ground function obeys <a id="set:count-mgf-bound"></a>$$\label{set:count-mgf-bound}
 \log\int e^{z f_A}\,\mathrm d\mu\le CzD|A|
               \qquad(0\le z\le1).$$ The constants are uniform for $K\ge100$, $D$ sufficiently large, and $\alpha\in I_\alpha$.*

</div>

<a id="set:count-tail"></a>
This is <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[H](#ref-H), Proposition 3.7 and Equation (3.18)]. For example, since $n_{B_v}\le e^{\sqrt3/2}f_{\{v\}}$, it gives $$\label{set:count-tail}
 \mu\{n_{B_v}>C_1D\}\le e^{-cD}$$ once $C_1$ is a sufficiently large constant. It also bounds every fixed moment of a count in a bounded region by $C_kD^k$. Hard packing gives $n_{B_v}\le C r^{-3}\le C D^3$. Consequently polynomial factors in local counts do not affect the negligibility of <a href="#set:count-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="set:count-tail">[set:count-tail]</a>. For an $R$-cell, a union bound involves only $O(R^3)$ unit cells. These observations justify removing bounded-count truncations in the moment estimates proved in Section <a href="#en:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="en:consequences">4</a>.

<a id="stmt-set-close-tail"></a>
<div id="set:close-tail" class="proposition">

**Proposition 7** (Joint close-neighbor tail). *For each particle label $i$ let $d_i(X)=\min_{k\ne i}d_K(x_i,x_k)$, with the value $+\infty$ if $N=1$. Given $\theta>0$, one can choose $b_*>0$ sufficiently small, and then $D$ sufficiently large, so that the set $$\mathcal Q(X)=\left\{v\in\mathcal L:
    \#\{i:d_K(x_i,v)\le5,\ d_i(X)\le b_*D^{-1/3}\}
          \ge\theta D\right\}$$ satisfies, for every $A\subset\mathcal L$, <a id="set:close-tail-bound"></a>$$\label{set:close-tail-bound}
 \mu\{A\subset\mathcal Q\}\le p_D^{|A|},
 \qquad
 p_D=\bigl(C_\theta b_*^{-2}D^{-4/3}\bigr)^{1/q_*}\longrightarrow0,$$ where $q_*$ is an absolute positive integer. The uniformity is the same as in Proposition <a href="#set:count-mgf" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="set:count-mgf">6</a>.*

</div>

This is <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[H](#ref-H), Proposition 3.7 and Equation (3.20)]. It remains true if the distance cutoff is decreased. Neighborhoods of any other fixed radius can be covered by finitely many radius-$5$ neighborhoods; reducing $\theta$ and taking a bounded coloring gives the corresponding joint bound. The constants may then depend on that fixed radius. All these estimates are under the original ground-state law. They are not assertions about a law conditioned on later path certificates.

<a id="en:section"></a>
### Local energy bounds with retained excitations

<span id="sec:energy" label="sec:energy"></span>

A scalar energy asymptotic does not by itself determine the one-body momentum distribution. We need a lower bound that retains the positive quadratic excitation operator as well as the leading energy. This section proves that bound on boxes of side $E$, with $E=B$ or $R$; Lemma <a href="#en:sharp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="en:sharp">10</a> is the precise low-excitation statement. Section <a href="#en:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="en:consequences">4</a> will compare the summed lower bound with the ground-state energy upper bound and extract count fluctuations and local one-body covariances. The remaining occupation of wavelengths comparable with the whole torus requires the spatial argument later in the paper.

Throughout this section, global expectations are in a normalized bosonic ground vector, with no sign or reality restriction. Local inequalities also apply to unnormalized box restrictions with spectator variables. For a cell $A$, write $n_A=\sum_{i=1}^N\mathbf 1_A(x_i)$ and $q_A=n_A/(D|A|)$. Constants are uniform for $\alpha$ in the fixed compact window of Section <a href="#set:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="set:section">2</a>; they may depend on the fixed exponents and, when indicated, on $j$.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The local energy and the relevant scales

<a id="en:upper"></a>
Put $c_{\rm L}=128/(15\sqrt\pi)$ and, with $r$ held fixed, define <a id="en:energy-density"></a>$$\label{en:energy-density}
 e_r(x)=4\pi r x^2+4\pi c_{\rm L}r^{5/2}x^{5/2}.$$ The hard-sphere upper bound of <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[BBCOS](#ref-BBCOS), Theorem 1.1], after rescaling, gives $$\label{en:upper}
 E_{N,K,r}\le V\{e_r(D)+O(D^{-c_*})\}$$ for an absolute $c_*>0$, in the order of limits just specified. That theorem uses periodic boxes and a fixed hard-core scattering length. To obtain this upper bound along a density-convergent sequence, one may fix a slightly higher physical density $\rho'>\rho$ and choose, in the same physical box, an integer $M\ge N$ with $M/L^3\to\rho'$. Slicing an $M$-particle trial state and then averaging over its omitted coordinates gives $E_{N,L,a}\le E_{M,L,a}$. The thermodynamic upper bound at $\rho'$ therefore bounds the limsup at $\rho$. Its explicit right side is continuous in $\rho'$, so one may let $\rho'\downarrow\rho$. Equivalently, one may use the sequence-independent thermodynamic energy limit. No uniform finite-volume rate is used here.

<a id="en:tangent"></a>
We use a generic box side $E\asymp Q=D^\theta$, where $\theta=u$ or $w$, and write $v_b=E^3$. Neumann restriction to the boxes lowers the energy. More explicitly, decompose configuration space into the sectors assigning each label to a box, restrict each coordinate integral to its assigned box, and discard exclusions between different boxes. This operation takes no derivative of a sector indicator. Variables assigned elsewhere remain spectators, so every local form inequality below may be integrated in those variables. The affine comparison energy for a box containing $n$ particles is $$\label{en:tangent}
 t_b(n)=v_b e_r(D)+e_r'(D)(n-Dv_b).$$ Its sum over all boxes is $Ve_r(D)$ in every assignment sector.

<a id="en:replacement"></a>
Use <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[FJGMOT](#ref-FJGMOT), Proposition 2.1] to choose a nonnegative radial bounded potential $v$ supported in the hard core. Its scattering length $a'$ and its scattering functions $\varphi$, $\omega=1-\varphi$, $g=v\varphi$ satisfy $$\label{en:replacement}
 0\le r-a'\le CrD^{-1}Q^{-1},\quad
 \|v\|_1\le CQ,\quad
 \|v\|_\infty\le D^{C},\quad
 \widehat g(0)=8\pi a',\quad
 \|g\|_1+\|g\omega\|_1\le C/D.$$ The replacement also satisfies the radial comparison condition (2.6) in that proposition. The Neumann operator with this potential is denoted by $H_b$. Its expectation on the original hard-core restriction equals the restricted kinetic energy, since $v$ vanishes outside the excluded region. It can subsequently be applied to excitation-localized vectors, which need not satisfy the hard-core condition.

<a id="en:middle-sector"></a>
The fixed factors in the box lengths matter for the particle-number hypothesis of <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[FJGMOT](#ref-FJGMOT)]. Use the choices <a href="#set:scale-factors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="set:scale-factors">[set:scale-factors]</a>–<a href="#set:nested-lengths" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="set:nested-lengths">[set:nested-lengths]</a>, so that $E=b_\theta D^\theta(1+o(1))$ with $b_\theta=\alpha_0^{-(1+3\theta)/2}$, in the specified order of limits. Define $$\rho_{\rm aux}
   =E^{-2/(1+\theta)}(a')^{-(1+3\theta)/(1+\theta)},\qquad
 K_\ell=(\rho_{\rm aux}(a')^3)^{-\theta/2}.$$ Then the source length relation $E=K_\ell/(\rho_{\rm aux}a')^{1/2}$ holds exactly. Moreover, $$\frac{\rho_{\rm aux}}D
 =\left(\frac{\alpha_0}{\alpha}\right)^{(1+3\theta)/(1+\theta)}
          (1+o(1))\in[1/3,3]$$ for sufficiently small $\theta$ and sufficiently large $D$, uniformly in the fixed $\alpha$ window. The change from $r$ to $a'$ only affects the $o(1)$ factor; the replacement bounds are chosen with fixed slack. Consequently every $$\label{en:middle-sector}
 Dv_b/2\le n\le6Dv_b$$ lies in the particle-number range $n\le20\rho_{\rm aux}v_b$ of that paper. Set <a id="en:parameters"></a>$$\label{en:parameters}
 K_H=K_\ell^{100},\qquad
 M\asymp Dv_bQ^{-400}.$$ Rounding $M$ to an integer changes no estimate. For sufficiently small $\theta$, the required inequalities are <a id="en:parameter-conditions"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 K_H\gg K_\ell^4,\qquad
 K_\ell K_H^3\ll(\rho_{\rm aux}(a')^3)^{-1/2},\qquad
 K_\ell^{5/4}K_H^2\ll(\rho_{\rm aux}(a')^3)^{-1/2},\label{en:parameter-conditions}\\
 1\ll M\ll E/a',\qquad
 M\ll \rho_{\rm aux}v_bK_H^{-3}K_\ell^{-17/4},\qquad
 K_H\ll\sqrt{E/a'}.\nonumber
\end{gathered}$$ For example, the ratio in the second upper bound for $M$ is $O(Q^{-95.75})$. All powers containing $D$ rather than $Q$ can be made favorable by decreasing $\theta$. We shall also require $u\ll c_*$ and several inequalities of this form below; they form a finite list.

Let $u_p$ be the normalized cosine basis of the Neumann cube, indexed by $p\in(\pi/E)\mathbb N_0^3$. Thus $$u_p(x)=v_b^{-1/2}\prod_{i=1}^3 c_{p_i}\cos(p_ix_i),\qquad
 c_0=1,\quad c_s=\sqrt2\ (s\ne0).$$ Write $a_p,a_p^*$ for the associated annihilation and creation operators, $n_+=\sum_{p\ne0}a_p^*a_p$, and <a id="en:bands"></a>$$\label{en:bands}
 \mathcal P_L=\{p:0<|p|\le K_H/E\},\quad
 n_L=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P_L}a_p^*a_p,\quad n_H=n_+-n_L.$$ All estimates on unnormalized vectors include their squared norm in every scalar error.

<a id="en:rough-section"></a>
#### A rough bound retaining kinetic energy and count fluctuations

The sharp box estimate will be used only after restricting the number of low-frequency excitations. We first establish a less accurate bound that pays for this restriction and controls its complementary part. The point of the construction is to use most of the kinetic energy only near another particle, leaving enough energy to control the low Neumann modes. We use Dyson’s radial replacement and the small-box energy method of Lieb and Yngvason <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Dyson](#ref-Dyson), [LY](#ref-LY)], keeping track of the unused kinetic energy explicitly.

Throughout this subsection let $\mathcal C=[0,E]^3$, $v_b=E^3$, and $E\asymp Q=D^\theta$. The exponent $\theta>0$ will be either of the two box exponents. We assume that it is sufficiently small, with bounds specified below. Let $r=\alpha/D$, where $\alpha$ ranges over the fixed compact window of positive values. The nonnegative radial replacement potential, denoted here by $v$, is supported in the ball of radius $r$. Its scattering length $a'$ obeys $$0\le r-a'\le CrD^{-1}Q^{-1},\qquad \|v\|_1\le CQ.$$ Write $$H_n=\sum_{i=1}^n(-\Delta_{\mathcal C,i}^{\mathrm N})
       +\sum_{1\le i<j\le n}v(x_i-x_j).$$ All inequalities below are quadratic-form inequalities. They hold with additional Hilbert-space-valued spectator variables. Let $A$ run over the cubes of side $d$ in a fixed subdivision of $\mathcal C$, and put $n_A=\sum_i\mathbf 1_A(x_i)$. The side $d$ is fixed while $D\to\infty$.

<a id="stmt-en-rough"></a>
<div id="en:rough" class="lemma">

<a id="en:rough-ineq"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Rough box bound). *<span id="en:rough-bound" label="en:rough-bound"></span>There are constants $c,C>0$ and an exponent $c_{\mathrm r}>0$, independent of the sufficiently small box exponent $\theta$, such that, for $0\le n\le6Dv_b$, $$\label{en:rough-ineq}
 \begin{split}
 H_n\ge {}&\frac{4\pi r n^2}{v_b}-Cv_bD^{1-c_{\mathrm r}}
       +c\,\mathrm d\Gamma\bigl(\min\{p^2,1\}\bigr)\\
 &\quad+cD\sum_A |A|\,
   \mathfrak h\left(\frac{n_A}{D|A|}-\frac{n}{Dv_b}\right),
 \qquad \mathfrak h(t)=\frac{t^2}{1+|t|}.
 \end{split}$$ Here $p$ denotes the Neumann frequency and $\mathrm d\Gamma$ sums the indicated one-particle operator over all particles. One may take $c_{\mathrm r}=10^{-3}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-en-rough"><summary>Proof of stmt-en-rough</summary>

*Proof.* We give separately the short-distance energy estimate and the recovery of the kinetic term. Choose equal subdivisions of every $d$-cube with side $\ell\asymp D^{-0.31}$, and set $$s\asymp D^{-0.36},\qquad \varepsilon_D=D^{-0.002}.$$ Rounding the number of subdivisions changes only fixed constants. For large $D$, the support radius $r$ is smaller than $s/2$. Restrict configuration space to sectors specifying which small cube contains each labeled particle. Restriction of an $H^1$ function to these sectors incurs no gradient cost; their faces carry Neumann form conditions. Discard interactions between different small cubes.

<a id="en:radial-scattering"></a>
*The radial replacement.* Fix one particle coordinate and all the others in its small cube. Partition that cube into Voronoi regions of the other particles, clipping at the cube boundary. Each region is star-shaped about its center. On a ray from a center, the scattering variational principle gives, for $R\ge r$, $$\label{en:radial-scattering}
 \int_0^R\left((1-2\varepsilon_D)|f'(t)|^2
           +\tfrac12v(t)|f(t)|^2\right)t^2\,\mathrm dt
       \ge (1-2\varepsilon_D)a'|f(R)|^2.$$ For completeness, with kinetic coefficient one the minimizer at fixed endpoint is the regular scattering solution divided by its endpoint value. Its minimum is $a_v(1-a_v/R)^{-1}|f(R)|^2$, where $a_v$ is the scattering length of the potential in the integrand. This follows by integrating the radial scattering equation against the minimizer; the boundary term is $R^2\overline{f(R)}f'(R)$. Completing the quadratic form around the minimizer proves the same lower bound for every ray function. Factoring out $1-2\varepsilon_D$ replaces $v$ by $v/(1-2\varepsilon_D)$, whose scattering length is at least $a'$, by the variational definition and monotonicity. Dropping the factor $(1-a_v/R)^{-1}\ge1$ proves <a href="#en:radial-scattering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:radial-scattering">[en:radial-scattering]</a>. This argument applies first to smooth functions and then by form approximation.

Let $U$ be constant on $s/2\le |x|\le s$, zero elsewhere, and normalized by $\int_{\mathbb R^3}U=4\pi$; thus $\int_0^\infty U(t)t^2\,\mathrm dt=1$. On a ray ending at distance $T$, average <a href="#en:radial-scattering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:radial-scattering">[en:radial-scattering]</a> over radii $R\le T$ with weight $U(R)R^2\,\mathrm dR$. The total weight is at most one, so only the energy at distances at most $s$ is used. Integrating the rays proves the lower bound $(1-2\varepsilon_D)a'U(d_{\mathrm{nn}})$, where $d_{\mathrm{nn}}$ is the nearest-neighbor distance within the small cube. If there is no other particle this expression is zero. Half of every pair interaction is assigned to each endpoint, so summing over the coordinates uses no interaction twice.

<a id="en:kinetic-reserve"></a>
More explicitly, let $S_i$ be the set of positions of coordinate $x_i$ within distance $s$ of another particle in its small cube. The preceding argument leaves the form $$\label{en:kinetic-reserve}
 \mathcal T_{\mathrm{rem}}[\psi]
 =\sum_i\int\left[\varepsilon_D+(1-2\varepsilon_D)\mathbf 1_{S_i^c}\right]
                    |\nabla_i\psi|^2.$$ There is also a separate reserve $\varepsilon_D\sum_i\|\nabla_i\psi\|^2$ available for the next step. Indeed, these two reserves and the coefficient $1-2\varepsilon_D$ used inside $S_i$ sum to the original kinetic coefficient at every point.

<a id="en:smallcube-mean"></a>
*The energy in a small cube.* The cases $k=0,1$ have zero scalar lower bound and are immediate. For $k\ge2$ particles in a cube of side $\ell$, put $$W_k=(1-2\varepsilon_D)a'\sum_{i=1}^kU(d_{\mathrm{nn},i}).$$ The first nonzero eigenvalue of the reserved kinetic operator is $\Delta_\ell=\varepsilon_D\pi^2\ell^{-2}$. Its normalized constant vector will be denoted by $\Omega_k$. For any fixed $C_0$, uniformly for $k\le C_0D\ell^3$, $$\label{en:smallcube-mean}
 \langle W_k\rangle_{\Omega_k}
 \ge\frac{4\pi(1-2\varepsilon_D)a'k(k-1)}{\ell^3}
       \left(1-C\frac{s}{\ell}
                 -C\frac{ks^3}{\ell^3}\right).$$ To see this, keep the first coordinate a distance at least $s$ from the cube boundary. For each possible nearest neighbor integrate that neighbor over the shell of radii $s/2,s$. The other $k-2$ points lie outside the corresponding ball with probability at least $1-Cks^3/\ell^3$, by the union bound. These events for different nearest neighbors are disjoint up to null sets. The removed boundary layer occupies at most $Cs/\ell$ of the cube volume. Summing over the first coordinate proves <a href="#en:smallcube-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:smallcube-mean">[en:smallcube-mean]</a>.

The multiplication operator satisfies $$0\le W_k\le Cka's^{-3},\qquad
 \langle W_k^2\rangle_{\Omega_k}
       \le Cka's^{-3}\langle W_k\rangle_{\Omega_k}.$$ The scalar mean in <a href="#en:smallcube-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:smallcube-mean">[en:smallcube-mean]</a> is less than $\Delta_\ell/2$ for large $D$. Temple’s inequality therefore gives <a id="en:smallcube-temple"></a>$$\label{en:smallcube-temple}
 \inf\operatorname{spec}\left(
    \varepsilon_D\sum_{i=1}^k(-\Delta_i^{\mathrm N})+W_k\right)
 \ge \langle W_k\rangle_{\Omega_k}
   -\frac{\langle W_k^2\rangle_{\Omega_k}}
          {\Delta_\ell-\langle W_k\rangle_{\Omega_k}}.$$ This version of Temple’s bound follows by decomposing the constant trial vector into the ground eigenspace and its orthogonal complement; the latter spectrum is at least $\Delta_\ell$, by the min–max principle and $W_k\ge0$, and the variance bounds its spectral weight. The powers used here are $$D\ell^3\asymp D^{0.07},\quad
 s/\ell\asymp D^{-0.05},\quad
 Ds^3\asymp D^{-0.08},\quad
 ka's^{-3}\le CD^{0.15},\quad
 \Delta_\ell\asymp D^{0.618}.$$ Consequently there is $\delta_D\le CD^{-0.002}$ such that the energy available in each small cube is bounded below by <a id="en:smallcube-quadratic"></a>$$\label{en:smallcube-quadratic}
   \frac{4\pi r}{\ell^3}(1-\delta_D)k(k-1),
       \qquad 0\le k\le C_0D\ell^3.$$ The replacement of $a'$ by $r$ is included in $\delta_D$.

<a id="en:smallcube-convex"></a>
For larger $k$, divide its labels into groups of sizes between $m/2$ and $m$, where $m=\lfloor C_0D\ell^3\rfloor$. Such a partition exists for $k>m$, by taking $\lceil k/m\rceil$ groups of nearly equal sizes; decreasing the lower endpoint by one accommodates integer rounding. Drop interactions between groups before applying the radial argument. A position close to a member of its group is also close to a particle of the original cube, so the reserve <a href="#en:kinetic-reserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:kinetic-reserve">[en:kinetic-reserve]</a> remains available. After reducing the harmless lower endpoint to $m/3$, the group bound is at least $$\frac{4\pi r}{\ell^3}(1-\delta_D)(m/3-1)k.$$ Choose once and for all $b>12$ and then $C_0>12b$. Define the convex function on $[0,\infty)$ $$F_b(y)=
 \begin{cases}
 y^2,&0\le y\le b,\\
 2by-b^2,&y>b.
 \end{cases}$$ Both the small-$k$ estimate and the group estimate imply the common bound $$\label{en:smallcube-convex}
 \frac{4\pi r}{\ell^3}(1-\delta_D)
 \left[(D\ell^3)^2F_b\left(\frac{k}{D\ell^3}\right)-k\right].$$ For $k\le m$ this follows from $F_b(y)\le y^2$. For $k>m$, use $F_b(y)\le2by$ and $m/3-1\ge2bD\ell^3-1$ for sufficiently large $D$.

<a id="en:convex-remainder"></a>
*From small-cube counts to the desired count penalty.* Set $\vartheta=n/(Dv_b)\in[0,6]$. For all $y\ge0$, $$\label{en:convex-remainder}
 F_b(y)\ge\vartheta^2+2\vartheta(y-\vartheta)
                         +c\mathfrak h(y-\vartheta).$$ If $y\le b$, the remainder after the tangent is $(y-\vartheta)^2$. If $y>b$, write $t=y-\vartheta$ and $a=b-\vartheta$; the remainder is $2at-a^2\ge at$, and $a\ge b-6>0$. These two observations prove <a href="#en:convex-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:convex-remainder">[en:convex-remainder]</a> with a uniform positive constant. Sum <a href="#en:smallcube-convex" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:smallcube-convex">[en:smallcube-convex]</a> over the small cubes. The linear tangent terms cancel because their counts sum to $n$. The subtracted self-count terms cost at most $$C r\ell^{-3}n\le Cv_bD^{0.93}.$$ The factor $1-\delta_D$ changes the leading scalar by at most $Cv_bD^{1-0.002}$. Since $\mathfrak h$ is convex, Jensen’s inequality within each $d$-cube gives $$\sum_{\text{small cubes }C\subset A}|C|\,
   \mathfrak h\left(\frac{n_C}{D|C|}-\vartheta\right)
 \ge |A|\,\mathfrak h\left(\frac{n_A}{D|A|}-\vartheta\right).$$ This proves the scalar and count terms in <a href="#en:rough-ineq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:rough-ineq">[en:rough-ineq]</a>, while leaving <a href="#en:kinetic-reserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:kinetic-reserve">[en:kinetic-reserve]</a> unused.

<a id="en:gradient-evaluation"></a>
*Recovery of the one-particle kinetic term.* We now sum the sector restrictions back on the original $H^1$ form domain of the $E$-cube; the following estimates concern its global one-coordinate slices, not an arbitrary function in the larger direct sum of the cut Neumann form domains. Let $u_p$ be the normalized cosine basis of the Neumann cube, with $p\in(\pi/E)\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^3$. The vector fields $\nabla u_p/|p|$, for $p\ne0$, are orthonormal. Let $\mathcal P$ be their projection for $0<|p|\le D^{0.003}$. The explicit cosine formulas give $$\label{en:gradient-evaluation}
 \|\mathcal P\|_{L^2\to L^\infty}^2
 \le\frac{C}{E^3}\#\{p:0<|p|\le D^{0.003}\}
 \le CD^{0.009}.$$ For a fixed slice in the other particle coordinates, enlarge $S_i$ to the union $S$ of the balls of radius $s$ about all the other particles. Its volume is at most $Cns^3$. The integral kernel bound in <a href="#en:gradient-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:gradient-evaluation">[en:gradient-evaluation]</a>, or duality, implies $$\|\mathcal P\mathbf 1_S F\|_2^2
       \le CD^{0.009}ns^3\|F\|_2^2
       \le CD^{-0.071+3\theta}\|F\|_2^2.$$ Thus, for $F=\nabla f$, $$\|\mathcal P\nabla f\|_2^2
 \le2\|\mathbf 1_{S^c}\nabla f\|_2^2
                +CD^{-0.071+3\theta}\|\nabla f\|_2^2.$$ For example, if $\theta<10^{-3}$, the last coefficient is $o(\varepsilon_D)$. Integration by parts, using the Neumann condition on $u_p$, gives $\langle\nabla u_p/|p|,\nabla f\rangle
   =|p|\langle u_p,f\rangle$. A fixed fraction of <a href="#en:kinetic-reserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:kinetic-reserve">[en:kinetic-reserve]</a> therefore controls $\sum_{0<|p|\le D^{0.003}}p^2|\langle u_p,f\rangle|^2$. Another fraction of the everywhere reserve controls the remaining weights one, since $\varepsilon_Dp^2\ge D^{0.004}$ for $|p|>D^{0.003}$. Sum the slice estimates over the coordinates. Together with the count bound already obtained this proves <a href="#en:rough-ineq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:rough-ineq">[en:rough-ineq]</a> with $c_{\mathrm r}=10^{-3}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="en:localization-section"></a>
#### Localization in the number of low-frequency excitations

The rough estimate now permits a cutoff in an excitation number, in the spirit of the large-matrix localization of <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[LS2001](#ref-LS2001), Appendix A], without losing an appreciable energy density. We record explicitly the form error because the cutoff need not preserve the hard-core constraint. It is applied only after passing to the bounded replacement potential.

<a id="en:three-cutoffs"></a>
Let $P_0=|u_0\rangle\langle u_0|$, $n_0=\mathrm d\Gamma(P_0)$, and $n_+=n-n_0$. Define $$P_L=\sum_{0<|p|\le K_H/E}|u_p\rangle\langle u_p|,
 \qquad n_L=\mathrm d\Gamma(P_L),\qquad n_H=n_+-n_L,$$ where $K_H\asymp Q^{100}$. Let $M\asymp Dv_bQ^{-400}$ be a positive integer. Choose real functions $f,h$ on $\mathbb R$ with $$f^2+h^2=1,\quad f=1\text{ on }(-\infty,M/8],\quad
 f=0\text{ on }[M/4,\infty),\quad
 \operatorname{Lip}(f)+\operatorname{Lip}(h)\le C/M.$$ For example, write $f=\cos\vartheta$, $h=\sin\vartheta$, where $\vartheta$ increases smoothly from zero to $\pi/2$ on this interval. Define $$\label{en:three-cutoffs}
 F_1=f(n_L)^2,\qquad F_2=h(n_L)^2,\qquad
 F_3=\sqrt2 f(n_L)h(n_L).$$ Then $\sum_{j=1}^3F_j^2=1$. The first and third localized vectors are supported on $n_L\le M/4$; the second is supported on $n_L\ge M/8$. We also have <a id="en:count-reconstruction"></a>$$\label{en:count-reconstruction}
 \psi=F_1\psi+F_2\psi,\qquad
 \|A\psi\|^2\le2\|AF_1\psi\|^2+2\|AF_2\psi\|^2$$ for every operator $A$ for which these expressions are defined. The second inequality is the reason for using three cutoffs: later it transfers configuration-count estimates without a commutation assumption.

<a id="stmt-en-localization"></a>
<div id="en:localization" class="lemma">

<a id="en:cutoff-form-error"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Form error of the excitation cutoff). *<span id="en:cutoff-error" label="en:cutoff-error"></span>Let $\psi$ belong to the form domain of $H_n$, and let $V_n=\sum_{i<j}v(x_i-x_j)$. Then $$\label{en:cutoff-form-error}
 \left|\sum_{j=1}^3\langle F_j\psi,H_nF_j\psi\rangle
                   -\langle\psi,H_n\psi\rangle\right|
 \le\frac{C}{M^2}\left[
     \langle\psi,V_n\psi\rangle
       +\|v\|_1\frac{K_H^3}{v_b}\,
                  n\langle\psi,n_+\psi\rangle\right].$$ For any one-particle operator $a$ with $\|a\|\le1$, the corresponding error for $\mathrm d\Gamma(a)$ is at most $CnM^{-2}\|\psi\|^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-en-localization"><summary>Proof of stmt-en-localization</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\Pi_k$ project onto $n_L=k$, and write $\psi_k=\Pi_k\psi$. The kinetic operator commutes with every cutoff. A two-particle multiplication operator changes $n_L$ by at most two, so $\Pi_kV_n\Pi_l=0$ for $|k-l|>2$. The difference of the potential forms is exactly $$-\frac12\sum_{k,l}\sum_{j=1}^3
       (F_j(k)-F_j(l))^2\langle\psi_k,V_n\psi_l\rangle.$$ The cutoffs have Lipschitz constants at most $C/M$. Positivity gives $$|\langle\psi_k,V_n\psi_l\rangle|
 \le\langle\psi_k,V_n\psi_k\rangle^{1/2}
      \langle\psi_l,V_n\psi_l\rangle^{1/2}.$$ Since each row has at most five nonzero blocks, the absolute form difference is at most <a id="en:dephased-error"></a>$$\label{en:dephased-error}
 \frac{C}{M^2}\langle\psi,\mathcal D(V_n)\psi\rangle,
 \qquad
 \mathcal D(V_n)=\sum_k\Pi_kV_n\Pi_k
       =\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}
          e^{-itn_L}V_ne^{itn_L}\,\mathrm dt.$$

<a id="en:pair-low-projection"></a>
The cosine evaluation bound on the low band is $\sup_x P_L(x,x)\le CK_H^3/v_b$. For each pair it implies $$\label{en:pair-low-projection}
 P_{L,i}v(x_i-x_j)P_{L,i}
       \le C\|v\|_1\frac{K_H^3}{v_b}P_{L,i}.$$ Indeed, condition on $x_j$ and all other coordinates, apply $|P_Lg(x_i)|^2\le(\sup_xP_L(x,x))\|P_Lg\|_2^2$, and integrate the potential in $x_i$. For the pair $(i,j)$, the rotations on the other coordinates cancel. Writing $z=e^{it}-1$, the remaining rotation is $$(1+zP_{L,i})(1+zP_{L,j})
   =1+zP_{L,i}+zP_{L,j}+z^2P_{L,i}P_{L,j}.$$ The squared norm after multiplication by $v_{ij}^{1/2}$ is bounded by a fixed constant times the sum of the four squared norms. Use <a href="#en:pair-low-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:pair-low-projection">[en:pair-low-projection]</a> on the last three terms, and $P_{L,i}P_{L,j}\le P_{L,i}$, to obtain $$\langle\psi,\mathcal D(V_n)\psi\rangle
 \le C\langle\psi,V_n\psi\rangle
      +C\|v\|_1\frac{K_H^3}{v_b}\,
                       n\langle\psi,n_L\psi\rangle.$$ This and $n_L\le n_+$ prove <a href="#en:cutoff-form-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:cutoff-form-error">[en:cutoff-form-error]</a>. A one-particle operator has block width one instead of two. Using its second-quantized norm at most $n$, and $2\|\psi_k\|\|\psi_l\|\le
\|\psi_k\|^2+\|\psi_l\|^2$, proves the final assertion. ◻

</details>

We apply the lemma to the restrictions of a normalized hard-core ground vector to box-assignment sectors. Its zero extension belongs to the soft-potential form domain, and the potential expectation is bounded by the hard-core energy. The localized vectors need not satisfy the hard-core constraint; all their lower bounds use $H_n$. The finite-rank cosine projections preserve $H^1$, so the localized vectors are in this form domain.

<a id="en:preliminary-excitations"></a>
Here is the quantitative energy accounting. Write $e_0(x)=4\pi rx^2$. Summing the rough bound and comparing with the thermodynamic upper bound $V(e_0(D)+O(1))$ gives, over boxes with $n\le6Dv_b$, $$\label{en:preliminary-excitations}
 \frac1V\sum\langle n_+\rangle
           \le CQ^2D^{1-c_{\mathrm r}}.$$ To justify using the tangent to $e_0$ in this summation for every particle sector, note first that $n\le6Dv_b$ gives the exact scalar remainder $4\pi r(n-Dv_b)^2/v_b$. If $n>6Dv_b$, partition the labels into groups of sizes between $3Dv_b-o(Dv_b)$ and $6Dv_b$, apply Lemma <a href="#en:rough-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="en:rough-bound">[en:rough-bound]</a> to each group and drop intergroup interactions. The energy per particle is then at least $12\pi\alpha-o(1)$, whereas the slope of the tangent is $8\pi\alpha$. These sectors have a positive excess of order $n$ above that tangent. Finally, $\min\{p^2,1\}\ge cE^{-2}$ for $p\ne0$, which proves <a href="#en:preliminary-excitations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:preliminary-excitations">[en:preliminary-excitations]</a>. All sums include the squared norms of sector vectors and any spectator variables.

We use the cutoffs only for $Dv_b/2\le n\le6Dv_b$. The total potential expectation is at most $CDV$, and $M\asymp DQ^{-397}$. Substitution of <a href="#en:preliminary-excitations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:preliminary-excitations">[en:preliminary-excitations]</a> into <a href="#en:cutoff-form-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:cutoff-form-error">[en:cutoff-form-error]</a> gives the explicit bound <a id="en:localization-density-error"></a>$$\label{en:localization-density-error}
 \frac1V\sum\left|\text{localization form error}\right|
 \le C\left(D^{-1}Q^{794}+D^{-c_{\mathrm r}}Q^{1097}\right)
 \le CQ^{-1},$$ provided $795\theta<1$ and $1098\theta<c_{\mathrm r}$. The one-particle observable error is bounded by the first term on the right. Thus these errors are negligible on the energy scale used below.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The sharp bound on a low-excitation piece

The next bound is the information beyond the scalar LHY formula that will determine the mesoscopic occupation. We give the passage from the published operator estimates to the exact vectors, including the zero-mode resolution and the cosine-basis convention.

<a id="en:bogoliubov"></a>
For $z\ne0$ put $\zeta_z=z/|z|$, and set $\zeta_0=1$. Define <a id="en:tau"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \tau(p)&=p^2-\frac\pi{2E^2}-\frac{K_H}{E^2}
                       \mathbf 1_{\{p\notin\mathcal P_L\}},\label{en:tau}\\
 \rho_z&=|z|^2/v_b,\qquad
 D_p(z)=\sqrt{\tau(p)^2+2\rho_z\widehat g(p)\tau(p)},\nonumber\\
 \beta_p(z)&=\frac{\tau(p)+\rho_z\widehat g(p)-D_p(z)}
                   {\rho_z\widehat g(p)},\qquad
 b_p(z)=\frac{a_p+\zeta_z^2\beta_p(z)a_p^*}{\sqrt{1-\beta_p(z)^2}}.
 \label{en:bogoliubov}
\end{aligned}$$ At zero numerator and denominator use the continuous value. The factor $\zeta_z^2$ restores the phase in the pairing term; for real $z$ it equals one. Put $\widetilde D_p=D_p$ on $\mathcal P_L$ and $\widetilde D_p=K_H^{-1}D_p$ on its complement. The square root is real: the support of $g$ gives $|\widehat g(p)-\widehat g(0)|\le Cr^3p^2$, and $\tau(p)\asymp p^2$. In particular the possible negative part of $\widehat g$ cannot destroy positivity when $D$ is large and $|z|^2\le2n$ in <a href="#en:middle-sector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:middle-sector">[en:middle-sector]</a>. More explicitly, $\rho_z\le CD$ implies $\tau(p)+2\rho_z\widehat g(p)\ge(c-CD^{-2})p^2$. At $\rho_z=0$ we have $D_p=\tau(p)$, $\beta_p=0$, and $b_p=a_p$.

For $n$ in <a href="#en:middle-sector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:middle-sector">[en:middle-sector]</a> and an $n$-particle vector $\psi$, let $\psi_z=(\langle z|\otimes1)\psi$ denote its zero-mode coherent projection, with measure $\mathrm d^2z/\pi$. Here $|z\rangle=e^{-|z|^2/2}\sum_{k\ge0}z^k|k\rangle/\sqrt{k!}$. Define the nonnegative quadratic expression <a id="en:bog-energy"></a>$$\label{en:bog-energy}
 \mathcal B(\psi)=\int_{|z|^2\le2n}
       \sum_{p\ne0}\widetilde D_p(z)\|b_p(z)\psi_z\|^2
                                  \frac{\mathrm d^2z}{\pi}.$$

<a id="en:coherent-tail"></a>
For later truncations we record the coherent tail at this point. There is no large-$z$ assumption on the original vector. Write $\psi=\sum_{k=0}^n|k\rangle\otimes\psi^{(n-k)}$. Angular integration shows that its radial coherent density is a sum of gamma densities $e^{-s}s^k/k!$, $0\le k\le n$, weighted by $\|\psi^{(n-k)}\|^2$. The gamma Chernoff bound consequently gives $$\label{en:coherent-tail}
 \int_{|z|^2>2n}(1+|z|)^A\|\psi_z\|^2
       \frac{\mathrm d^2z}{\pi}\le C_A n^{A/2}e^{-cn}\|\psi\|^2$$ for every fixed $A\ge0$. The local lower bound we shall prove is the following.

<a id="stmt-en-sharp"></a>
<div id="en:sharp" class="lemma">

<a id="en:sharp-bound"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Energy with a retained quasiparticle term). *For $n$ in <a href="#en:middle-sector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:middle-sector">[en:middle-sector]</a> and a bosonic $n$-particle vector $\psi$ in the form domain of $H_b$ satisfying $\psi=\mathbf 1_{\{n_L\le M/4\}}\psi$, $$\label{en:sharp-bound}
 \langle\psi,H_b\psi\rangle
 \ge\{v_be_r(n/v_b)-Cv_bQ^{-1/4}\}\|\psi\|^2
       +cE^{-2}\langle n_++K_Hn_H\rangle_\psi
       +c\mathcal B(\psi).$$ The constants are uniform in the particle sector and in spectator variables.*

</div>

Its proof must preserve two positive terms: the gap controlling physical excitation numbers, and the coherent quadratic energy $\mathcal B$. Write $S=E^{-2}(\pi n_+/2+K_Hn_H)$. The symmetrization and second-quantization formulas of <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[FJGMOT](#ref-FJGMOT), Theorem 2.6 and Lemma 2.8] separate the number-operator block $\widehat g(0)\{n(n-1)-n_+(n_+-1)\}/(2v_b)$ from terms containing the constant mode. Coherent resolution of the latter terms gives the quadratic–cubic operator $\mathcal H_0(z)$ defined below, with the upper-symbol corrections accounted for in the proof of Lemma <a href="#en:sharp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="en:sharp">10</a>. We first bound this operator while retaining its excitation energy, then estimate the correction to the cosine coefficients, and finally return to the box Hamiltonian.

<a id="en:printed-cubic"></a>
<a id="en:H0"></a>
The next operator estimate specifies the gap fractions in the energy analysis of <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[FJGMOT](#ref-FJGMOT)]. Write $\mathcal P_L^{\mathbb Z}$ for the signed version of the low band, extend operator subscripts by componentwise absolute value, put $c_p=\prod_i c_{p_i}$, and let $c(p,k)=\prod_i c_{k_i-p_i}/(c_{p_i}c_{k_i})$ be the printed coefficient in that paper. Define $$\begin{aligned}
 Q_3(z)&=\frac1{v_b}\sum_{\substack{p\in\mathcal P_L^{\mathbb Z},\ k\ne0\\
                                      |p-k|\ne0}}
 c(p,k)\widehat g(k)(\overline z a_p^*a_{|p-k|}a_k+\mathrm{h.c.}),
                                                        \label{en:printed-cubic}\\
 \mathcal H_0(z)&=\sum_{p\ne0}\left[
   (\tau(p)+\rho_z\widehat g(p))a_p^*a_p
   +\frac{\widehat g(p)}{2v_b}
      (\overline z^2a_p^2+z^2a_p^{*2})\right]
   +\frac{\widehat{g\omega}(0)|z|^4}{2v_b}\nonumber\\
 &\quad+\rho_z\sum_{p\ne0}
       \{\widehat{g\omega}(0)+\widehat{g\omega}(p)\}a_p^*a_p+Q_3(z).
                                                        \label{en:H0}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $k$ ranges over the nonnegative Neumann lattice, and $|p-k|$ in an operator subscript means componentwise absolute value. Thus $\mathcal H_0$ is (2.36) of <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[FJGMOT](#ref-FJGMOT)] at zero chemical parameter with its artificial scalar convexity term subtracted. The exact cosine coefficients are treated separately in Lemma <a href="#en:cosine-correction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="en:cosine-correction">12</a>.

<a id="stmt-en-small-gap"></a>
<div id="en:small-gap" class="lemma">

<a id="en:small-gap-bound"></a>
**Lemma 11** (A small-gap version of the quadratic–cubic bound). *Let $n$ satisfy <a href="#en:middle-sector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:middle-sector">[en:middle-sector]</a>, $|z|^2\le2n$, and $\xi=\mathbf 1_{\{n_+\le n,\ n_L\le M/4\}}\xi$. For every fixed $\delta>0$ and sufficiently large $D$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \langle\xi,\mathcal H_0(z)\xi\rangle
 &\ge 4\pi c_{\rm L}(a')^{5/2}v_b\rho_z^{5/2}\|\xi\|^2
       +\sum_{p\ne0}\widetilde D_p(z)\|b_p(z)\xi\|^2
       -\delta\langle S\rangle_\xi
       -C_\delta v_bQ^{-1/4}\|\xi\|^2 .
                                                        \label{en:small-gap-bound}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-en-small-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-en-small-gap</summary>

*Proof.* We use the Bogoliubov identity (2.39), the completion of a square (7.18)–(7.20), and the scalar integral estimate in <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[FJGMOT](#ref-FJGMOT), Lemma A.1].
