 \frac{CC_\omega}{R^2}
       \sum_{0\le s<h_0}(s+m+1)^2
 \le CC_\omega\frac{H^3}{R^2},\qquad m\le\delta_1H.$$ Combining <a href="#rt:root-global" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rt:root-global">[rt:root-global]</a>, <a href="#rt:bulk-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rt:bulk-tail">[rt:bulk-tail]</a>, and <a href="#rt:endpoint-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rt:endpoint-tail">[rt:endpoint-tail]</a> in <a href="#rt:tail-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rt:tail-expansion">[rt:tail-expansion]</a>, and using $\sum_m(Ap)^m(m+1)^2<\infty$, proves <a href="#rt:general-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rt:general-tail">[rt:general-tail]</a>.

Every vertex used in $J_*$ has distance greater than $h-q$ from $z$. Apply <a href="#rt:general-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rt:general-tail">[rt:general-tail]</a> with $H=h-q$, adjusting the fixed lower threshold on $R$. Since $h\asymp R^{1/2}$, $$H^{1-2\sigma}+H^3/R^2+e^{-cH}
 \le C\bigl(R^{-(\sigma-1/2)}+R^{-1/2}\bigr)
 \le CR^{-c_g}.$$ This proves <a href="#rt:rare" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rt:rare">[rt:rare]</a>.

Finally, viewed from the terminal endpoint, a nominal walk has the form $$u_{n-i}=y+\left(-i,
       \left\lfloor\frac{-i\Delta_2}{n}\right\rfloor
                         +S^{(2)}_{s_n(i)},
       \left\lfloor\frac{-i\Delta_3}{n}\right\rfloor
                         +S^{(3)}_{s_n(i)}\right).$$ The integer identity $\lfloor(n-i)\Delta_a/n\rfloor-\Delta_a
 =\lfloor-i\Delta_a/n\rfloor$ verifies this formula. The same two independent processes, bounded speed, clamped alignment, and component charges apply. This proves the common-terminal-end version and completes Proposition <a href="#rt:routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rt:routes">32</a>.

<a id="stmt-rt-averaging"></a>
<div id="rt:averaging" class="remark">

*Remark 35*. Both <a href="#rt:exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rt:exponential">[rt:exponential]</a> and <a href="#rt:rare" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rt:rare">[rt:rare]</a> average over the environment. They do not assert the corresponding estimates for each fixed datum. They remain valid with any further retention event inserted on their left sides. In applications where endpoint weights depend on the datum, a deterministic pointwise bound by $CR^{-6}$ permits replacing those weights before the environment average; the counting proof of <a href="#rt:endpoint-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rt:endpoint-tail">[rt:endpoint-tail]</a> then applies unchanged.

</div>

<a id="long:section"></a>
### Transport between distant smoothed deletion measures

<span id="sec:long-palm" label="sec:long-palm"></span>

The local comparison controls variations inside a mesoscopic cell. We now compare the smoothed deletion measures of two distant $R$-cells. The desired estimate is a squared $L^2$ distance $o(D^{-1})$ between their square-root densities $s_v$ and $s_w$. It follows from a common measure whose likelihood is close to one in both augmented references.

The transport uses a route whose length may grow with $K$, so a fixed density expense per step would be useless. Instead we estimate the second moment by comparing two transports with the same first tag. When they change disjoint sets of labels, deleting the checks between them separates their conditional integrals; the expense of restoring these checks is determined by route encounters. Random label choices also make shared output coordinates rare. Encounters at the common first tag are unavoidable. We keep an initial portion unchanged in the observed copy and integrate out its partners in the other copy. Encounters confined to these silent portions then incur no expense; encounters involving their seams are included in the count $J_*$ of Proposition <a href="#rt:routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rt:routes">32</a>. The use of unnormalized conditional integrals extends the common-measure comparison of <a id="pm-cite-68"></a>[[H](#ref-H), Section 8]; the sequential pinning and the estimates that approach one are proved here.

Write $\mathcal A_{K,R}$ for the ordered pairs $(v,w)$ of $R$-cells whose centres have forward first-coordinate displacement in $[.30K,.45K]$. All averages over this set are normalized counting averages. An error $o_K(1)$ below tends to zero at fixed dilution along the thermodynamic sequence, uniformly over the normalized nonnegative ground vectors under consideration. Constants may depend on the parameters fixed before $D$, including $x$ and $j$, but never on $K$ or on a route length.

<a id="stmt-long-comparison"></a>
<div id="long:comparison" class="proposition">

<a id="long:hellinger"></a>
**Proposition 36** (Distant comparison of deletion measures). *For sufficiently small $x>0$ relative to the previously fixed exponents, the functions $s_v$ of Lemma <a href="#palm:reference" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:reference">24</a> satisfy $$\label{long:hellinger}
 \frac1{|\mathcal A_{K,R}|}\sum_{(v,w)\in\mathcal A_{K,R}}
       \int |s_v(Y')-s_w(Y')|^2\,\mathrm dY'
       =o(D^{-1})+o_K(1).$$ The $o(D^{-1})$ is uniform over the nonnegative ground vectors and over the fixed window of $\alpha$. The thermodynamic error is taken to zero first.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### The two-copy construction

We retain the notation of Section <a href="#palm:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:section">6</a>: $\mathcal D$ is the exposed data, $\mathcal I$ the unexposed labels, $B_i$ their plain bridge laws, and $\lambda$ the conditional admissible bridge law. For an $R$-cell $v$, put $T_v=f_0D|v|$, $h_{v,i}=\mathbf 1_{\{X_i\in v\}}$, and $T_v^{\rm rel}=T_v^{-1}\sum_{i\ {\rm flagged}}h_{v,i}$. The initial mass assigned to distinct tags $i,j$ is <a id="long:tag-weight"></a>$$\label{long:tag-weight}
             \frac{h_{v,i}h_{w,j}}{T_vT_w}.$$ Only tags in $\mathcal I$ and the retained data events of Lemma <a href="#palm:omissions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:omissions">26</a> will be used. In particular the two cells have bad-free neighborhoods of radius a sufficiently large constant times $R$.

<a id="long:label-atom"></a>
To apply Proposition <a href="#rt:routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rt:routes">32</a>, choose fixed lattice-cube enlargements $Q_v,Q_w$ containing every candidate tag mean-site for the respective cells. Their sides are at most $C R$, and their $10R$ margins lie in the retained bad-free neighborhoods. Let $z$ be the first tag’s mean-site and put $Q=Q_w$. The forward displacements of the cell centres lie in $[.30K,.45K]$, so those from $z$ to all sites in $Q$ lie in $[.29K,.46K]$ when $K/R$ is sufficiently large. Retain the route event $\mathcal E$ of that proposition as well as the endpoint events already specified. Conditional on the data and the two mean-sites, sample its route using fresh randomness. Its vertices are distinct good sites. At each interior site choose one available interior label uniformly, excluding the tags. Choices at different sites are independent. If a bounded number of additional exclusions is necessary, make it before the uniform choice. The supply estimate gives the bound $$\label{long:label-atom}
       \sup_l\Pr(\text{chosen label}=l\mid\mathcal D,
                         \text{route and tags})
                  \le \frac{C}{Df_0}.$$ This gives a list of distinct labels $i_0=i,i_1,\ldots,i_m=j$. Consecutive bridge means have bounded distance. The route is simple, so at most a fixed number of list entries have means in any fixed-radius ball. This bound does not depend on $m$.

Let $H_0=\lfloor R^{1/2}\rfloor$. The silent prefix consists of the vertices up to and including the last vertex within $H_0$ of the first endpoint. Define the silent suffix by reversing the route. Proposition <a href="#rt:routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rt:routes">32</a> shows that these pieces are disjoint and have length $O(H_0)$. Write their list indices as $0\le k<a_0$ and $a_1<k\le m$. Keep the prefix trajectories unchanged in the left copy and the suffix trajectories unchanged in the right copy. All trajectories outside the list are unchanged in both copies.

The remaining paths are generated in the following order.

1.  For $a_0\le k\le a_1+1$, generate a pair consisting of the left path of $i_k$ and the right path of $i_{k-1}$. Fix a unit cell at bounded distance from both means as a measurable function of the exposed endpoints alone, before sampling any latent or proposed paths. Choose the common midpoint uniformly in that cell, and use the two independent pinned bridge laws given that midpoint. Condition on individual cutoffs, compatibility with every path retained in that copy, and compatibility with earlier middle pairs. Require also midpoint $s$-separation from these opponents. The probability of this event is at least $1/2$, by Proposition <a href="#lp:insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:insertion">21</a>; thus each step is a probability kernel. Fix any deterministic order for these middle steps.

<a id="long:pin-divisor"></a>
2.  For the remaining pairs, one member is already present as a retained original. If its midpoint $y$ is within $8b$ of the mean of the label to be inserted and is $s$-separated from all paths already present in the insertion copy, generate the other member with its pinned bridge law. Impose all individual and compatibility checks, and divide this trial measure by $$\label{long:pin-divisor}
                      \max\{1/2,p\},$$ where $p$ is its total success probability. If either preliminary midpoint test fails, use the zero measure. Use a fixed deterministic order within each end. Prefix pins occur in the right copy; suffix pins occur in the left copy. These kernels have mass at most one.

Every generated path satisfies the constraints of its own copy. The left midpoint of $i_k$ is the right midpoint of $i_{k-1}$ for every $1\le k\le m$. After deleting $i$ on the left and $j$ on the right the midpoint multisets therefore coincide. Order these baths with one common uniform permutation. The weighted construction defines a finite common measure $\nu_{vw}$ on the bath observation, with one lift to each augmented reference.

<figure id="fig:transport">
<embed src="figures/transport.pdf" />
<figcaption>The midpoint pairing, shown schematically for $m = 8$, $a_{0} = 3$ and $a_{1} = 5$. Each link identifies the left midpoint of $i_{k}$ with the right midpoint of $i_{k − 1}$. The middle pairs receive fresh common midpoints. At each end one original trajectory is retained and its partner is pinned through the retained midpoint. After deleting the crossed first left and last right tags, all remaining midpoints are paired. The rows show label order, not geometric positions or paths.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="stmt-long-mass"></a>
<div id="long:mass" class="lemma">

<a id="long:mass-bound"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Mass of the transport). *For some $c_3>0$, the mass of this common measure satisfies, after averaging over $(v,w)\in\mathcal A_{K,R}$, $$\label{long:mass-bound}
 \nu_{vw}(1)\ge
       \mathbb E[T_v^{\rm rel}T_w^{\rm rel}]-C D^{-1-c_3}-o_K(1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-long-mass"><summary>Proof of stmt-long-mass</summary>

*Proof.* The omitted tags, failed endpoint neighborhoods and route-existence rejections have total weighted mass $O(D^{-1-c})+o_K(1)$ by Lemma <a href="#palm:omissions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:omissions">26</a> and Proposition <a href="#rt:routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rt:routes">32</a>. For the latter use the marginal bad-site bound in an $O(R^3)$ neighborhood, the exponentially small probability of a filled component reaching across the margin, and the thermodynamically vanishing probability of a large component. Multiplying by the bounded number of relative-count factors costs at most $D^{Cx}$, outside events smaller than any power of $D$.

It remains to check the subprobability steps near the two ends. Use the full-path count and motion event of Proposition <a href="#lp:good-probability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:good-probability">19</a> in the enlarged endpoint neighborhoods solely to estimate the discarded mass; it is not added to the conditional bridge law. Its ordinary-original motion tests give $\sup_q|\omega(q)-Y|\le b$ and $\sup_q|\omega(q)-Z|\le b$ in the chosen lift. In particular its midpoint $y$ has distance at most $b$ from the source mean. Consecutive means have distance bounded by a fixed constant, which is at most $b$ after increasing the fixed cutoff constant. Thus every required pin has $|y-m_{\rm insert}|\le2b<8b$. The full-path event fails with weighted mass smaller than $D^{-1-c}$ after the $O(R^3)$ union bound. On this event, a pinned trial with a separated midpoint has probability at least $3/4$. Indeed, start with the full original obstacle collection in Proposition <a href="#lp:insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:insertion">21</a>, delete any replaced originals, and add the already generated paths. Only boundedly many added paths can meet the trial neighborhood, by simplicity of the route. The last assertion of that lemma applies to these additions.

For a middle path the necessary separation from a later pinned midpoint was already checked in the copy containing the retained original that provides that midpoint. The paired middle paths have the same midpoint. The two silent ends are separated by a distance of order $K$, so no check joins them for sufficiently large $K$. Thus the only remaining separation failure is that a selected silent original has midpoint within $s$ of another original midpoint. In each bounded region the expected number of such ordered pairs is at most $CD^2s^3$. Summing the selected interior labels uses <a href="#long:label-atom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:label-atom">[long:label-atom]</a>; summing an end tag uses its factor $T_v^{-1}$ or $T_w^{-1}$. Enlarging all site sums to the endpoint neighborhoods and using the other relative-count bounds gives at most $$C R^3D^{Cx}\frac{D^2s^3}{Df_0}
       \le C D^{-7/5+3w+C'x}.$$ The midpoint range failure is already covered by the displacement cutoffs chosen above. Outside these discarded events every pin has $p\ge3/4$, so <a href="#long:pin-divisor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:pin-divisor">[long:pin-divisor]</a> equals $p$ and the pin kernel has mass one. Since $w$ and then $x$ were chosen small, all displayed losses have a strict power saving beyond $D^{-1}$. This proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### Conditional likelihoods and the checks between two transports

We next prove absolute continuity of the common measure’s augmented lifts and bound their second moments. Denote the left lift density by $\ell_{v,w}$. Its reference has mass $\mathbb ET_v^{\rm rel}$, while its own mass is $\nu_{vw}(1)$. The mass estimate therefore shows why the leading term sought for its second moment is $\mathbb E[T_v^{\rm rel}(T_w^{\rm rel})^2]$: in the centered second moment the three leading terms combine to $$\mathbb E[T_v^{\rm rel}(T_w^{\rm rel})^2]
 -2\mathbb E[T_v^{\rm rel}T_w^{\rm rel}]+\mathbb ET_v^{\rm rel}
 =\mathbb E[T_v^{\rm rel}(T_w^{\rm rel}-1)^2].$$ Lemma <a href="#palm:counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:counts">25</a> controls this last expression. The task below is to bound the remaining second-moment error independently of route length.

For a fixed route and label list let $S=\{i_k:k\ge a_0\}$ be the changed labels in the left copy. The retained first tag $i$ is not in $S$. Strip its factor $h_{v,i}/T_v$, but keep the factor $h_{w,j}$ from the original second tag. Denote the resulting likelihood by $L_S$. The division by $T_w$ and all choices will be averaged at the end.

<a id="long:unnormalized-output"></a>
<a id="long:exact-likelihood"></a>
Here is an explicit integral defining $L_S$. For fixed outside paths $o$ let $A_S(x;o)$ be the indicator of all reference checks incident to $S$, including individual cutoffs, and let $$Z_S(o)=\int A_S(x;o)\,\mathrm dB_S(x),\qquad
 B_S=\bigotimes_{l\in S}B_l.$$ Let $K_S(x,o;\mathrm dz)$ be the subprobability kernel of the complete two-copy construction, observed only in its changed left coordinates. Its input $x$ consists of the latent original $S$ paths. Define the finite output measure $$\label{long:unnormalized-output}
 M_S(o;\mathrm dz)=\int A_S(x;o)h_{w,j}(x)
                         K_S(x,o;\mathrm dz)\,\mathrm dB_S(x).$$ On reference-admissible configurations, $$\label{long:exact-likelihood}
                         L_S(z,o)=\frac{\mathrm dM_S(o)}{\mathrm dB_S}(z).$$ To verify this, both the conditional input law and the conditional reference output law have the same divisor $Z_S(o)$. It cancels in their likelihood ratio, as in Lemma <a href="#palm:likelihood" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:likelihood">28</a>. Absolute continuity follows from the ordinary densities of middle outputs and the distinct latent midpoint sources for pinned left outputs. A detailed density bound is proved below.

For upper bounds, integrate out all right pins against the silent prefix. They occur after the middle steps and are subprobability kernels. The left suffix pins do not use these right paths. Removing these right kernels therefore increases <a href="#long:unnormalized-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:unnormalized-output">[long:unnormalized-output]</a>. In the rest of the upper-bound argument $K_S$ denotes this enlarged kernel. This operation is the reason that meetings confined to two silent prefixes cost nothing.

Fix two route and label choices with the same first tag, with changed sets $S,T$ and second tags $j,j'$. Fix the paths outside $U=S\cup T$, at a value where the regular conditional reference law is defined. Every statement until the averaging subsection is uniform in these fixed allowed values. Let $Z_U$ be the reference normalizer on $U$ relative to $B_U$.

Define the separate $S$ protocol by deleting the old paths indexed by $T\setminus S$ from its outside obstacles and removing each check against such a path. Keep its list of generated transitions unchanged. In particular a right middle path keeps the endpoints of its label even when that label’s old trajectory has been deleted as a left obstacle. Define the separate $T$ protocol symmetrically. Use a superscript minus for these protocols and write $A_S^-,Z_S^-,M_S^-$ for their reference indicator, reference normalizer and unnormalized output measure.

These separate quantities depend on their own latent and output variables and on the common fixed outside of $U$. They do not depend on the other set’s unshared path variables. The only apparent exception is the first right middle insertion at the edge of a silent prefix. Its old trajectory is retained on the left, but its right insertion uses only the fixed endpoints; all checks using the old trajectory are among the deleted outside checks. The right pins have already been integrated out. This verifies the asserted independence also at that edge.

We now specify exactly which comparisons can contribute a cost. Each reference check between a label of $S\setminus T$ and a label of $T\setminus S$ is an edge of a finite graph on these two sets. Each deleted proposal check gives a second kind of edge: one end records its generated transition and the other the removed old opponent label. A transition has at most two generated paths, one in each copy. A label appears as a primary path once and as an auxiliary path at most once. The fixed confinement radii imply that a check is identically satisfied unless the associated route sites have distance at most a fixed $r_c$. For a predecessor or a midpoint source use the neighboring route site. Increase $r_c$ once to include these bounded displacements.

Let $J$ be the full site-contact count of Proposition <a href="#rt:routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rt:routes">32</a>, and $J_*$ its contact count between the changed portions from the common end, with these step neighbors included. There are at most $CJ_*$ edges in the graphs just defined, hence at most $CJ$ edges. Every degree is bounded by a constant: a simple lattice route contains only boundedly many vertices in an $r_c$-ball. The constants in this assertion do not count the route length.

<a id="long:midpoint-cost"></a>
<a id="long:worst-cost"></a>
For a check between distinct labels $l,k$ put <a id="long:beta"></a>$$\label{long:beta}
 \beta_{lk}=b_D\left(1+\frac1{s\vee d(Y_l,Y_k)}
                       +\frac1{s\vee d(Z_l,Z_k)}\right),
 \qquad b_D=\frac{C(\log D)^C}{D}.$$ For a pinned trial with midpoint $y$ checked against an old path $X_k$, also put $$\label{long:midpoint-cost}
                 m(y,X_k)=\frac{b_D}{s\vee d(y,X_k(0))}.$$ All compared labels are distinct, even when the sets $S,T$ intersect: an outside obstacle is never the label currently inserted. Lemma <a href="#lp:collision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:collision">20</a> bounds each corresponding collision or uniform-midpoint separation cost by $C\beta_{lk}$, and a pinned collision by $C(\beta_{lk}+m(y,X_k))$ when the pin satisfies separation. Since $s=D^{-4/5}$, all these costs are at most $CD^{-19/100}$ for large $D$. Let $B_{ST}$ be the sum of the $\beta$ costs over all affected checks, with their bounded multiplicities, and let $M_{ST}$ be the corresponding sum of <a href="#long:midpoint-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:midpoint-cost">[long:midpoint-cost]</a>. Thus $$\label{long:worst-cost}
      B_{ST}+M_{ST}\le C D^{-19/100}J.$$

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Reference normalizers and proposal probabilities

There are two different sources of cost in the product of likelihoods. Checks between distinct labels prevent the two conditional integrals from factoring; the next two lemmas compare the reference normalizers and the proposal probabilities when these checks are removed. If a label belongs to both changed sets, its output coordinate is also shared by the two likelihoods. We will treat those coordinates separately, by bounding selected output marginals.

The normalization calculation uses one-path insertion bounds even when the number of changed paths is large. No joint normalizer is bounded below by a constant independent of that number.

<a id="stmt-long-normalizers"></a>
<div id="long:normalizers" class="lemma">

<a id="long:disjoint-normalizers"></a>
<a id="long:shared-normalizers"></a>
<a id="long:tag-integral-difference"></a>
**Lemma 38** (Reference normalizers). *If $S\cap T=\varnothing$, then $$\label{long:disjoint-normalizers}
       1\le\frac{Z_S^-Z_T^-}{Z_U}
                    \le \exp(CB_{ST}).$$ If $q=|S\cap T|$ is arbitrary, then $$\label{long:shared-normalizers}
       \frac{Z_S^-Z_T^-}{Z_U}
                    \le C^q\exp(CB_{ST}).$$ All normalizers are strictly positive. In the disjoint case, if $H$ is any measurable function of the latent originals with $0\le H\le1$, and $A^- = A_S^- A_T^-$, then $$\label{long:tag-integral-difference}
 0\le \frac1{Z_U}\int H(A^- - A_U)\,\mathrm dB_U
                       \le C e^{CB_{ST}} B_{ST}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-long-normalizers"><summary>Proof of stmt-long-normalizers</summary>

*Proof.* The conditional distribution of any one original path, given all the others, is bounded by $c_0^{-1}B_l$ with $c_0=3/4$, by Proposition <a href="#lp:insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:insertion">21</a>. This remains true after arbitrary checks have been removed. Every reduced normalizer is positive by inserting its coordinates successively; the resulting lower bound may depend on their number and is used only for positivity.

Suppose first that $S,T$ are disjoint. With their cross checks removed the integral factorizes as $Z_S^-Z_T^-$. Add the cross checks in any fixed order. For a check between $l$ and $k$, condition on all paths except $l$. Its failure probability is at most $c_0^{-1}\beta_{lk}$. Consequently this insertion multiplies the inverse normalizer by at most $$(1-c_0^{-1}\beta_{lk})^{-1}
                         \le \exp(2c_0^{-1}\beta_{lk})$$ for large $D$. Multiplication gives <a href="#long:disjoint-normalizers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:disjoint-normalizers">[long:disjoint-normalizers]</a>. Under the normalized product with all cross checks deleted, their union fails with probability at most $c_0^{-1}\sum\beta_{lk}$. Dropping $H\le1$ and using the normalizer ratio proves <a href="#long:tag-integral-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:tag-integral-difference">[long:tag-integral-difference]</a>.

For general $S,T$, put $A=S\cap T$. Delete the labels of $A$ and their incident checks from both separate reference integrals. This increases $Z_S^-$ and $Z_T^-$. The remaining sets $S\setminus A,T\setminus A$ are disjoint, so the preceding comparison applies to them. Their joint normalizer is at most $c_0^{-q}Z_U$, since one can add the $q$ labels in $A$ using their uniform insertion lower bounds. The cross-check costs used for the disjoint remainder are among those in $B_{ST}$. These two inequalities prove <a href="#long:shared-normalizers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:shared-normalizers">[long:shared-normalizers]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-long-transitions"></a>
<div id="long:transitions" class="lemma">

<a id="long:factor-exponential"></a>
<a id="long:factor-increment"></a>
**Lemma 39** (Comparison of transition factors). *Use the same latent originals and the same generated variables in the original and separate protocols. The original integrand is bounded by the separate integrand times a nonnegative factor $\mathcal R$ satisfying $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal R&\le e^{C D^{-19/100}J},
                                  \label{long:factor-exponential}\\
 \mathcal R&\le 1+C e^{C D^{-19/100}J}(B_{ST}+M_{ST}).
                                  \label{long:factor-increment}
\end{aligned}$$ The assertion is for the product of the two protocols as well. A transition with no removed check has ratio exactly one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-long-transitions"><summary>Proof of stmt-long-transitions</summary>

*Proof.* Fix the history before one generated pair or pin. Its proposal law before checks is unchanged by deletion of outside obstacles. Let $p$ and $p^-$ be the acceptance probabilities with and without the removed checks; then $p\le p^-$. For a middle pair $p\ge1/2$, and hence $$\frac{p^-}{p}\le 1+2(p^- -p).$$ For a pin, use $w=\max(1/2,p)$ and $w^- =\max(1/2,p^-)$. The map $p\mapsto\max(1/2,p)$ is increasing and $1$-Lipschitz, so $$\frac{w^-}{w}\le 1+2(p^- -p).$$ The ratio of admissibility indicators is bounded by one in this upper comparison. A midpoint separation indicator outside the pin proposal can also be increased. On the support of the old nonzero integrand, its midpoint is separated from every old opponent, so the pinned collision estimate applies. Off that support the inequality is automatic. The capped expression <a href="#long:midpoint-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:midpoint-cost">[long:midpoint-cost]</a> thus gives a valid bound everywhere.

For a middle proposal the union bound gives $p^- -p\le C\sum\beta_{lk}$ over its removed checks. For a pin the bound is $C\sum(\beta_{lk}+m(y,X_k))$. The proposal may be a coupled pair, but the removed checks involve one of its two marginals, and the uniform-midpoint insertion bounds apply to those marginals. Conditioning the original proposal does not enter this estimate of $p^- -p$.

Multiplying the displayed ratio bounds over the affected transitions of both protocols gives $\mathcal R\le\exp(C(B_{ST}+M_{ST}))$. The inequalities $e^a-1\le ae^a$ and <a href="#long:worst-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:worst-cost">[long:worst-cost]</a> give both conclusions. Unaffected transitions have identical numerators and divisors at the same history. Thus the number of factors actually paid is bounded by the number of graph edges, not by the number of generated transitions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Marginals of selected output paths

Before multiplying likelihoods with shared labels, we record the precise form of reverse integration used for their common output coordinates. Only primary marginals of a coupled pair are bounded. Its full two-copy law has equal midpoints and is not dominated by a product of free bridges.

<a id="stmt-long-kernel"></a>
<div id="long:kernel" class="lemma">

<a id="long:reverse-kernel"></a>
**Lemma 40** (Selected outputs of sequential kernels). *Let $\mu(\mathrm dx)$ be a probability measure of latent variables, followed by a finite ordered list of subprobability kernels. At stage $r$ the kernel generates a primary variable $z_r$ and arbitrary auxiliary variables. Let $Q$ be a set of stages. Suppose that, for every latent input and every allowed preceding history, the primary marginal at $r\in Q$ is at most $A_r\nu_r(x,\mathrm dz_r)$, where $\nu_r$ is a subprobability kernel depending on $x$ but not on the preceding history. Then the joint measure of the selected primary outputs is at most $$\label{long:reverse-kernel}
       \left(\prod_{r\in Q}A_r\right)
                   \int\mu(\mathrm dx)\prod_{r\in Q}\nu_r(x,\mathrm dz_r).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-long-kernel"><summary>Proof of stmt-long-kernel</summary>

*Proof.* Integrate an arbitrary nonnegative measurable test function of the selected outputs. Starting with the last stage, integrate every unselected stage using its mass bound one. At a selected stage integrate its auxiliary variables and use the primary marginal bound. The new test function depends on the latent input and the earlier selected outputs, but no longer on the history variables generated at this stage. Induction backwards through the finite list proves <a href="#long:reverse-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:reverse-kernel">[long:reverse-kernel]</a>. The argument first applies to bounded tests and then to all nonnegative tests by monotone convergence. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-long-shared-density"></a>
<div id="long:shared-density" class="lemma">

<a id="long:marginal-bound"></a>
**Lemma 41** (Uniform marginal bound). *Divide the separate output measure $M_S^-$ by its reference normalizer $Z_S^-$, and omit its original tag indicator if desired. For any $q$ specified primary output labels, its marginal is dominated by $$\label{long:marginal-bound}
                 G_D^q\bigotimes_{l\text{ specified}} B_l,
              \qquad G_D=C D^{C\sqrt x}.$$ The same assertion holds for $T$, and the constants are independent of the number of unselected outputs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-long-shared-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-long-shared-density</summary>

*Proof.* The normalized separate latent-original law is its admissible product bridge law. A middle stage, conditional on all these originals and on the preceding history, has primary marginal at most $C D^{Cx}B_l$, by Corollary <a href="#lp:trial-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:trial-density">22</a> and its acceptance probability at least $1/2$. A pin has primary conditional bridge law at most $2\mathbf 1_{\rm range}(y) B_l(\mathrm dz\mid z(0)=y)$, where $y$ is the midpoint of its retained original partner and $\mathbf 1_{\rm range}(y)=\mathbf 1_{\{|y-m_l|\le8b\}}$; all separation and avoidance indicators are discarded in this upper bound, so the retained cutoff is independent of the preceding history. This cutoff conditional bridge is the kernel $\nu_r$ in Lemma <a href="#long:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:kernel">40</a>. Both estimates hold uniformly in the history, including histories obtained after deleting some outside checks.

Apply Lemma <a href="#long:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:kernel">40</a> to the specified stages. For each specified pin its source is a latent original in $S$: these sources lie in the right-silent suffix. The sources for distinct pins are distinct labels. If there are $p$ specified pins, their joint latent-source marginal is bounded by $C^p$ times the corresponding product bridge law, by Corollary <a href="#lp:trial-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:trial-density">22</a>. Indeed the uniform conditional one-path density bound survives integration over any other latent coordinates and can then be iterated over these $p$ coordinates.

Let $a(l)$ be the source label of the pin with output label $l$. Integrating out all parts of its source other than the midpoint gives $g_{a(l)}(y)\,\mathrm dy$. On the retained range, neighboring means and the Gaussian ratio bound <a href="#lp:midpoint-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:midpoint-ratio">[lp:midpoint-ratio]</a> give $g_{a(l)}(y)\le C D^{C\sqrt x}g_l(y)$. Therefore $$\int \mathbf 1_{\rm range}(y)g_{a(l)}(y)
           B_l(\mathrm dz\mid z(0)=y)\,\mathrm dy
                         \le C D^{C\sqrt x}B_l(\mathrm dz).$$ Use this for each distinct source. Together with the middle-output bounds it proves <a href="#long:marginal-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:marginal-bound">[long:marginal-bound]</a>. No assertion of independence between a pin and its source midpoint has been used: the midpoint is integrated against the conditional bridge before the resulting free bridge bound is obtained. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### The product of two likelihoods

<a id="stmt-long-product"></a>
<div id="long:product" class="lemma">

<a id="long:disjoint-product"></a>
<a id="long:shared-product"></a>
**Lemma 42** (Disjoint and shared changed sets). *At almost every fixed admissible outside of $U=S\cup T$, the likelihoods of the two original transports satisfy the following bounds. If $S,T$ are disjoint, then $$\label{long:disjoint-product}
 \mathbb E[L_SL_T\mid U^c]\le
 \mathbb E[h_{w,j}h_{w,j'}\mid U^c]
 +C e^{C D^{-19/100}J}
          \bigl(B_{ST}+J_*b_D(1+t^{-3/2})\bigr).$$ If $q=|S\cap T|\ge1$, then $$\label{long:shared-product}
          \mathbb E[L_SL_T\mid U^c]
                     \le e^{C D^{-19/100}J}G_D^q,$$ after increasing the fixed constant in $G_D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-long-product"><summary>Proof of stmt-long-product</summary>

*Proof.* By <a href="#long:exact-likelihood" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:exact-likelihood">[long:exact-likelihood]</a> and the enlargement of the kernels, the conditional product integral is bounded by the integral using the free output measure $B_U$, the union admissibility indicator, and the single divisor $Z_U$. In each likelihood keep a separate set of latent original variables. These copies are not identified even if $S,T$ have common labels. Only shared output coordinates are identified by the integration against $B_U$.

Suppose first that the sets are disjoint. Apply Lemma <a href="#long:transitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:transitions">39</a>, delete the union output-admissibility indicator in an upper bound, and integrate with the separate protocols. The term one in <a href="#long:factor-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:factor-increment">[long:factor-increment]</a> has integral at most $$\frac1{Z_U}
       \int A_S^-(x)A_T^-(x')h_{w,j}(x)h_{w,j'}(x')
                              \,\mathrm dB_S(x)\mathrm dB_T(x').$$ Here the generated kernels were integrated using their mass bounds one. By <a href="#long:tag-integral-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:tag-integral-difference">[long:tag-integral-difference]</a> this is at most the reference expectation of $h_{w,j}h_{w,j'}$, plus $Ce^{CB_{ST}}B_{ST}$. The terms from $B_{ST}$ in <a href="#long:factor-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:factor-increment">[long:factor-increment]</a> are bounded by the same normalizer ratio and the masses of the separate normalized protocols, each at most one.

Consider one term $m(y,X_k)$ of $M_{ST}$. Its midpoint $y$ belongs to a latent original of one separate protocol, whereas $X_k$ is an output path of the other. Conditional on the fixed outside the two extended protocol measures are independent. On the side providing $y$, drop its tag indicator and integrate all generated transitions. The marginal of the source original is bounded by a fixed constant times its free bridge. For every fixed point $z$, the Gaussian midpoint density then gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{d(y,z)\le C_b}\frac{g_l(y)}{s\vee d(y,z)}\,\mathrm dy
 &\le \|g_l\|_\infty\int_{|u|\le1}|u|^{-1}\,\mathrm du
            +\int_{d(y,z)>1}g_l(y)\,\mathrm dy\\
 &\le C(1+t^{-3/2}).
\end{aligned}$$ The local range $C_b$ suffices because all other checks vanish; nearby lifts or the periodized Gaussian give the same estimate. This calculation does not require a density bound for the opposing output midpoint. There are at most $CJ_*$ such terms. Use <a href="#long:disjoint-normalizers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:disjoint-normalizers">[long:disjoint-normalizers]</a> for the normalizer ratio and absorb its exponent using <a href="#long:worst-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:worst-cost">[long:worst-cost]</a>. This proves <a href="#long:disjoint-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:disjoint-product">[long:disjoint-product]</a>.

For intersecting sets use <a href="#long:factor-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:factor-exponential">[long:factor-exponential]</a>. After dropping union admissibility, write the separate output measures as $Z_S^-f_S B_S$ and $Z_T^-f_T B_T$, with the original tag indicators included. Integrating unshared output coordinates leaves the product of their marginals on $A=S\cap T$. By Lemma <a href="#long:shared-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:shared-density">41</a>, one of these marginal densities is at most $G_D^q$; the other has integral at most one. Thus the free output integral is at most $Z_S^-Z_T^-G_D^q$. Division by $Z_U$, <a href="#long:shared-normalizers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:shared-normalizers">[long:shared-normalizers]</a>, and absorption of $C^q$ in $G_D^q$ prove <a href="#long:shared-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:shared-product">[long:shared-product]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### Averaging labels, routes and endpoints

We now return to the original augmented reference and its left lift density $\ell_{v,w}$. It is zero on omitted first tags or rejected data. On retained data and first tag $i$ it is the sum of $L_S/T_w$ over the candidate second tags, averaged over the route and interior-label choices. Squaring this sum produces exactly two independent choices with common first tag $i$ and the same underlying data. In particular the data are never independently resampled when the likelihood is squared.

<a id="stmt-long-label-average"></a>
<div id="long:label-average" class="lemma">

<a id="long:label-error"></a>
**Lemma 43** (Endpoint sums and label matches). *At fixed retained data and route sites, the label average of the error in <a href="#long:disjoint-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:disjoint-product">[long:disjoint-product]</a>, together with the contribution of all choices having $S\cap T\ne\varnothing$, is at most $$\label{long:label-error}
                C D^{-1+\zeta(x)}J_*e^{\delta_D J},
             \qquad \delta_D\longrightarrow0,
             \qquad \zeta(x)\longrightarrow0\quad(x\downarrow0).$$ This estimate includes the normalized sums over the three tag labels at their specified mean-sites. The remaining weight of each tag site is at most $CR^{-3}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-long-label-average"><summary>Proof of stmt-long-label-average</summary>

*Proof.* Every possible mean-site of an end tag lies in the corresponding $R$-cell enlarged by a fixed radius. At each site the number of eligible tags is at most $Cf_0D$, by the endpoint count estimate. Since $T_v=T_w=f_0DR^3$, their counting sums with $h\le1$ are dominated by a site weight $CR^{-3}$ and a label subprobability measure with maximum atom $C/(Df_0)$. These are deterministic upper bounds at the fixed data; the site weights will be removed before any environmental expectation. Zero or very small tag families cause no problem: their extracted label measure simply has mass less than one. Interior selections are probability measures and have the same maximum-atom bound <a href="#long:label-atom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:label-atom">[long:label-atom]</a>.

<a id="long:endpoint-average"></a>
For any fixed endpoint $z$, a dyadic decomposition of the endpoint count bound in Definition <a href="#lp:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:certificate">18</a> gives, for a candidate label family in a bounded neighborhood, $$\label{long:endpoint-average}
 \frac{C}{Df_0}\sum_l\frac1{s\vee d(z,Y_l)}
          \le C\left(1+\frac{D^{1/100}}{Df_0s}\right)\le C,$$ and the same holds for the $Z$ endpoints. To see the first inequality, sum separately the innermost ball of radius $s$ and the shells $2^as<d\le2^{a+1}s\le C_b$. The volume term sums as a geometric series of order $Df_0$, whereas the additive $D^{1/100}$ term sums to $CD^{1/100}/s$. Here $D^{1/100}/(Df_0s)=D^{-19/100+x}$ is bounded for small $x$.

Every edge contributing a $\beta$ cost has a label choice on at least one side which is free to average: the common first tag is outside both changed sets. For an interior label use <a href="#long:endpoint-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:endpoint-average">[long:endpoint-average]</a> conditional on the tags; for an end-tag label use the same bound in its candidate family at its fixed mean-site. Coincidence exclusions may be removed in this positive upper bound, because the reciprocal distance is capped at $s$. Thus each averaged $\beta$ costs at most $Cb_D$, and there are at most $CJ_*$ edges. The midpoint cost in <a href="#long:disjoint-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:disjoint-product">[long:disjoint-product]</a> has already been integrated. This bounds the disjoint error by $$C e^{C D^{-19/100}J}J_*b_D(1+t^{-3/2}).$$

For the intersecting choices let $\mathcal E$ be all pairs of slots in the two changed lists whose label choices could agree. Its cardinality is at most $CJ_*$, with all such pairs among the $CJ$ full contacts. Because each list has distinct labels, the actual matches form a matching: no slot belongs to two of them. The product label measure assigns each prescribed set of $h$ disjoint matches mass at most $(C/(Df_0))^h$. For interior matches, this follows by conditioning on the tags and then on one list, and using independence between sites in the other list. A tag can match an interior slot only at its one possible base site. For a match of the two second tags, sum one tag with the same maximum-atom bound. There are only two noncommon tag slots, so their exclusions change these bounds by a fixed factor per prescribed match, independent of the route length.

The tag measures have mass at most one, so summing the remaining tag variables cannot increase this bound.

Expand $$G_D^q=\prod_{e\in\mathcal E}
             \bigl(1+(G_D-1)\mathbf 1_{\{e\ {\rm matched}\}}\bigr).$$ To restrict to at least one match, designate one matched edge and bound the remaining product in the same way. The preceding probability bound and the elementary inequality $(1+a)^M\le e^{aM}$ give <a id="long:match-sum"></a>$$\label{long:match-sum}
 \mathbb E_{\rm labels}[\mathbf 1_{\{q>0\}}G_D^q]
       \le \frac{C G_D}{Df_0}J_*
                         \exp\left(\frac{C G_D}{Df_0}J\right).$$ Use this in <a href="#long:shared-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:shared-product">[long:shared-product]</a>. Since $G_D/(Df_0)=C D^{-1+x+C\sqrt x}$, the coefficient in this last exponent tends to zero. Absorb the fixed powers of $\log D$ in an arbitrarily small power depending on $x$, and combine the two estimates. They have the form <a href="#long:label-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:label-error">[long:label-error]</a>, with for example $\delta_D=C D^{-19/100}+C G_D/(Df_0)$. This proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="long:route-average"></a>
The next step is the only place where the environment is averaged in the likelihood estimate. The two routes share that environment. For each deterministic common endpoint, Proposition <a href="#rt:routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rt:routes">32</a> supplies $$\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\rm retained}e^{\kappa J}]\le C,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E_{\rm sites}\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\rm retained}\mathbf 1_{\{J_*>0\}}]
                                                \le CR^{-c_g}.$$ The second average is over the two independent noncommon sites with weights at most $CR^{-3}$ each. The deterministic bounds in Lemma <a href="#long:label-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:label-average">43</a> allow precisely this site average even though the actual candidate tag counts depend on the environment. For large $D$, $J^2e^{2\delta_DJ}\le C_\kappa e^{\kappa J}$. As $J_*\le J$, Cauchy–Schwarz consequently gives $$\label{long:route-average}
 \mathbb E_{\rm sites}\mathbb E[
     \mathbf 1_{\rm retained}J_*e^{\delta_DJ}]
                      \le C R^{-c_g/2}.$$ Uniformity in the common endpoint permits its remaining average as well. Combining <a href="#long:label-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:label-error">[long:label-error]</a> and <a href="#long:route-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:route-average">[long:route-average]</a> bounds the averaged likelihood error by $C D^{-1+\zeta(x)-wc_g/2}$. Choose $x$ small enough that $\zeta(x)<wc_g/4$. This has a strict power saving beyond $D^{-1}$.

<a id="stmt-long-augmented-moments"></a>
<div id="long:augmented-moments" class="proposition">

<a id="long:second-moment"></a>
**Proposition 44** (Augmented moments). *For some $c_4>0$, the left augmented likelihood satisfies $$\label{long:second-moment}
 \int\ell_{v,w}^2\,\mathrm dP_{{\rm aug},v}
       \le \mathbb E[T_v^{\rm rel}(T_w^{\rm rel})^2]
                           +CD^{-1-c_4}+o_K(1)$$ after averaging over $\mathcal A_{K,R}$. Its mass satisfies <a href="#long:mass-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:mass-bound">[long:mass-bound]</a>, and the same estimates hold with $v,w$ exchanged.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-long-augmented-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-long-augmented-moments</summary>

*Proof.* In the expansion of the squared likelihood keep the common first-tag factor $h_{v,i}/T_v$. Conditional on the outside of $S\cup T$ it is fixed, because neither changed set contains $i$. Apply Lemma <a href="#long:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:product">42</a> to the two choices. The conditional expectation in its disjoint leading term is exactly the original expectation of the two end-tag indicators. All tag choices, route choices, and interior label choices are fixed independently of the latent paths, conditional on the data. We may therefore sum this leading term on the original path space. Removing all good-event and noncoincidence restrictions in this nonnegative upper bound yields $$\mathbb E\left[
    \left(\sum_i\frac{h_{v,i}}{T_v}\right)
    \left(\sum_j\frac{h_{w,j}}{T_w}\right)^2\right]
             =\mathbb E[T_v^{\rm rel}(T_w^{\rm rel})^2].$$ The errors and intersecting choices are controlled by Lemma <a href="#long:label-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:label-average">43</a> and <a href="#long:route-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:route-average">[long:route-average]</a>. This proves <a href="#long:second-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:second-moment">[long:second-moment]</a>. The mass bound is Lemma <a href="#long:mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:mass">37</a>. For the right augmented estimate keep the same two-copy construction, and hence the same common measure. Reverse only the list indexing and designate the right copy primary; retain the actual chronological order of the generated middle kernels. Its common endpoint is then the last tag and its silent prefix is the previous silent suffix. The kernels pinned into the left copy can now be integrated out. The remaining right pins do not use them: the two end families lie in opposite copies, and no check joins the separated endpoint neighborhoods. Every kernel estimate and the reverse-integration lemma hold for an arbitrary fixed middle order, so this change of designation requires no resampling or reversal of the sampling order.

The original retention also gives the event required for the reversed route comparison. The fixed endpoint cube $Q_v$ and its $10R$ margin are bad-free. For every filled bad component, the actual first endpoint lies outside its fill by the original route event. Since $Q_v$ is nearest-neighbor connected and contains that endpoint, a path in $Q_v$ cannot enter the fill without meeting the bad component. Thus every site of $Q_v$ lies outside every such fill. The component-size cutoff is unchanged, so Proposition <a href="#rt:routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rt:routes">32</a> applies with the common terminal endpoint and the noncommon endpoints in $Q_v$. The preceding calculation therefore gives the right augmented estimates for the original common measure. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-long-augmented-moments-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-long-augmented-moments</summary>

<a id="long:centered-second"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#long:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:comparison">36</a>.* The augmented reference has mass $\mathbb ET_v^{\rm rel}$, while its common measure has mass $\int\ell_{v,w}\,\mathrm dP_{{\rm aug},v}$. Subtract twice the mass lower bound from the second-moment upper bound and add the reference mass. Proposition <a href="#long:augmented-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:augmented-moments">44</a> gives $$\label{long:centered-second}
 \int|\ell_{v,w}-1|^2\,\mathrm dP_{{\rm aug},v}
 \le \mathbb E[T_v^{\rm rel}(T_w^{\rm rel}-1)^2]
                                      +o(D^{-1})+o_K(1)$$ in the normalized pair average. The likelihood remains zero on the omitted reference events; their loss was included in the mass estimate.

Outside the negligible count tails, $T_v^{\rm rel}\le C/f_0$. Lemma <a href="#palm:counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:counts">25</a> and the uniform marginal weights of the ordered cell pairs therefore bound the main term of <a href="#long:centered-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:centered-second">[long:centered-second]</a> by $$C D^x\bigl(D^{-1-w/6}+D^{-1+x}R^{-3}\bigr)
     \le C\bigl(D^{-1-w/6+x}+D^{-1+2x-3w}\bigr)
                                      =o(D^{-1}).$$ The removed count tails remain negligible after multiplication by the relative counts, by the bookkeeping in Lemma <a href="#palm:counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:counts">25</a>. Choose $x<w/12$, in addition to its preceding restrictions. The same centered bound holds in the right augmented reference.

For finite measures $P$ and $\nu\ll P$ with likelihood $\ell$, their squared Hellinger distance is $\int(1-\sqrt\ell)^2\,\mathrm dP\le\int(1-\ell)^2\,\mathrm dP$. Projection cannot increase this distance: conditional Cauchy–Schwarz increases the affinity and preserves both masses. Project both augmented comparisons to the common ordered bath. Their reference densities are $s_v^2$ and $s_w^2$, by Lemma <a href="#palm:reference" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:reference">24</a>, and their common projected density is that of $\nu_{vw}$. The triangle inequality in $L^2$ and the two bounds <a href="#long:centered-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:centered-second">[long:centered-second]</a> now imply <a href="#long:hellinger" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="long:hellinger">[long:hellinger]</a>. Every volume-dependent rejection was recorded as $o_K(1)$ at fixed dilution; taking that limit before $D\to\infty$ completes the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="asm:section"></a>
### Control of long-wavelength excitations

<span id="sec:assembly" label="sec:assembly"></span>

The local energy estimate controls excitations outside the space of functions constant on $R$-cells. We now use the two deletion comparisons to show that the remaining excitations, between that space and the single constant orbital, have density $o(1)$. The distant comparison makes cell root-mean-squares nearly constant; the local fourth-moment comparison relates those root-mean-squares to cell averages. This gives the estimate needed to pass from local observables to global momentum tests.

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### A variance bound from distant pairs

We first remove the restriction on the pairs of $R$-cells in Proposition <a href="#long:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:comparison">36</a>. This is a statement about arrays in a Hilbert space and has no ground-state assumption.

<a id="stmt-asm-far-pairs"></a>
<div id="asm:far-pairs" class="lemma">

<a id="asm:far-variance"></a>
**Lemma 45** (Distant pairs control the variance). *Let $\mathcal H$ be a complex Hilbert space and let $f_z\in\mathcal H$, $z\in(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)^3$. Set $$I_M=\{h\in\{0,\ldots,M-1\}:.30M\le h\le .45M\},
 \qquad \overline f=M^{-3}\sum_z f_z.$$ For all sufficiently large $M$, there is an absolute constant $C$ such that $$\label{asm:far-variance}
 \frac1{M^3}\sum_z\|f_z-\overline f\|_{\mathcal H}^2
 \le \frac{C}{M^3|I_M|M^2}
      \sum_z\sum_{h_1\in I_M}\sum_{h_2,h_3\in\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z}
             \|f_{z+h}-f_z\|_{\mathcal H}^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-asm-far-pairs"><summary>Proof of stmt-asm-far-pairs</summary>

*Proof.* Use the unitary discrete Fourier transform on $(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)^3$. The right-hand quadratic form before multiplication by $C$ has Fourier multiplier $$a_M(k)=\frac1{|I_M|M^2}
   \sum_{h_1\in I_M}\sum_{h_2,h_3}
       |e^{2\pi i k\cdot h/M}-1|^2.$$ The variance on the left has multiplier one for $k\ne0$ and zero at $k=0$, so it suffices to bound $a_M(k)$ below away from zero for $k\ne0$. If $k_2$ or $k_3$ is nonzero modulo $M$, orthogonality gives $a_M(k)=2$. Otherwise, write $q=\min(k_1,M-k_1)$. For $q\ge1$, the geometric-sum formula and $\sin(\pi q/M)\ge2q/M$ give $$\left|\frac1{|I_M|}\sum_{h\in I_M}e^{2\pi i k_1h/M}\right|
        \le \frac{C_0}{q}.$$ Thus $a_M(k)\ge1$ when $q\ge2C_0$. For each of the finitely many remaining positive $q$, the common value $a_M(q,0,0)=a_M(M-q,0,0)$ converges to $$\frac1{.15}\int_{.30}^{.45}|e^{2\pi i q t}-1|^2\,\mathrm dt>0.$$ Their minimum is positive, uniformly for large $M$. Parseval’s identity now proves <a href="#asm:far-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:far-variance">[asm:far-variance]</a>, including Hilbert-valued arrays. ◻

</details>

<a id="asm:rms-variance"></a>
For a fixed nonnegative ground vector $\Phi$ and a cell $A\in\mathcal C_d\cup\mathcal C_R$, the function $s_A$ of Lemma <a href="#palm:reference" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:reference">24</a> belongs to $\mathcal H=L^2(\Lambda_K^{N-1},\mathrm dY)$. Explicitly, <a id="asm:rms"></a>$$\label{asm:rms}
 s_A(Y)=\left(\frac V{|A|}\int_A\Phi(y,Y)^2\,\mathrm dy\right)^{1/2}.$$ Applying Lemma <a href="#asm:far-pairs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="asm:far-pairs">45</a> with $M=K/R$ to Proposition <a href="#long:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="long:comparison">36</a> gives $$\label{asm:rms-variance}
 \frac1{|\mathcal C_R|}\sum_{A\in\mathcal C_R}
    \left\|s_A-\frac1{|\mathcal C_R|}\sum_{A'}s_{A'}\right\|_2^2
       =o(D^{-1})+o_K(1).$$ The uniformity and order of limits are those of Section <a href="#set:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="set:section">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### From root-mean-squares to arithmetic means

The occupation involves an integral of $\Phi$, whereas $s_A$ is a root-mean-square of $\Phi$. The next elementary inequality explains why the fourth-moment local comparison is enough to bridge this difference.

<a id="stmt-asm-rms-interpolation"></a>
<div id="asm:rms-interpolation" class="lemma">

<a id="asm:rms-inequality"></a>
<a id="asm:sqrt-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 46** (RMS interpolation). *For any finite nonempty array of nonnegative numbers $y_i$, with uniform averaging denoted by $\mathbb E_i$, put $a=\mathbb E_i y_i^2$ and $m=\mathbb E_i y_i$. Then $$\label{asm:rms-inequality}
 (\sqrt a-m)^2\le\frac12\mathbb E_{i,i'}
           \frac{(y_i-y_{i'})^4}{y_i^2+y_{i'}^2}.$$ The quotient is defined to be zero when its denominator is zero. Furthermore, for every $y,z\ge0$, $$\label{asm:sqrt-comparison}
 |\sqrt y-\sqrt z|^4
 \le\frac{(y-z)^4}{y^2+z^2}
 \le8|\sqrt y-\sqrt z|^4.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-asm-rms-interpolation"><summary>Proof of stmt-asm-rms-interpolation</summary>

*Proof.* Let $Q$ denote the double average on the right of <a href="#asm:rms-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:rms-inequality">[asm:rms-inequality]</a>, before its factor $1/2$. Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz and independence of the two uniform indices give $$[2(a-m^2)]^2
 =\bigl[\mathbb E_{i,i'}(y_i-y_{i'})^2\bigr]^2
 \le Q\mathbb E_{i,i'}(y_i^2+y_{i'}^2)=2aQ.$$ If $a>0$, divide by $4(\sqrt a+m)^2\ge4a$; the case $a=0$ is immediate. For the second statement factor $y-z=(\sqrt y-\sqrt z)(\sqrt y+\sqrt z)$. The remaining ratio is $(\sqrt y+\sqrt z)^4/(y^2+z^2)$, which lies between $1$ and $8$. ◻

</details>

<a id="asm:all-local-pairs"></a>
For any two $d$-cells $z,z'$ write $$Q_{z,z'}(Y)=
   \frac{|s_z(Y)-s_{z'}(Y)|^4}{s_z(Y)^2+s_{z'}(Y)^2}.$$ For a nearest-neighbor edge $e=(z,z')$, abbreviate this as $Q_e$. Inside one $R$-cell, any two $d$-cells can be joined by a path of at most $3R/d$ nearest-neighbor steps that stays in the cell. Put $t_z(Y)=\sqrt{s_z(Y)}$. If the path has $m$ edges, then <a href="#asm:sqrt-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:sqrt-comparison">[asm:sqrt-comparison]</a> and Hölder’s inequality give $$Q_{z,z'}\le8|t_z-t_{z'}|^4
       \le8m^3\sum_{e=(v,w)\text{ on the path}}|t_v-t_w|^4
       \le8m^3\sum_{e\text{ on the path}}Q_e.$$ Enlarging the last sum to all internal edges yields <a id="asm:path-fourth"></a>$$\label{asm:path-fourth}
 Q_{z,z'}(Y)\le C_jR^3
       \sum_{e\text{ internal edge of the }R\text{-cell}}Q_e(Y).$$ The full internal edge sum bounds every pair, so no estimate of path multiplicities is needed. Averaging over pairs and over $R$-cells, and using $|\mathcal C_d|/|\mathcal C_R|=(R/d)^3$, consequently gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\frac1{|\mathcal C_R|}\sum_{A\in\mathcal C_R}
   \frac1{|\{z\in\mathcal C_d:z\subset A\}|^2}
    \sum_{z,z'\subset A}\int Q_{z,z'}\,\mathrm dY
 \notag\\
 &\hspace{20mm}\le C_jR^6\,
       \frac1{|\mathcal C_d|}\sum_{e\text{ a nearest-neighbor edge}}
             \int Q_e\,\mathrm dY
 \notag\\
 &\hspace{20mm}\le C_j R^6 D^{-1-u/8}+o_K(1)
 =o(D^{-1})+o_K(1).
 \label{asm:all-local-pairs}
\end{aligned}$$ The second inequality is Proposition <a href="#palm:local-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="palm:local-comparison">23</a>. The last uses $6w<u/8$, which follows from <a href="#set:exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="set:exponents">[set:exponents]</a>. For fixed dilution the factor $R^6$ is fixed, so it causes no difficulty for the thermodynamic remainder.

<a id="asm:field-means"></a>
<a id="asm:micro-mean-rms"></a>
Define $$\label{asm:field-means}
 F(y,Y)=\sqrt V\Phi(y,Y),\qquad
 m_A(Y)=\frac1{|A|}\int_A F(y,Y)\,\mathrm dy.$$ Spatial integrals of $F$ over the whole torus will use the normalized measure $\mathrm dy/V$. For each cell, nonnegativity of $F$ implies $0\le m_A\le s_A$, and $$(s_A-m_A)^2\le s_A^2-m_A^2
       =\frac1{|A|}\int_A|F-m_A|^2\,\mathrm dy.$$ It follows from <a href="#en:d-occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:d-occupation">[en:d-occupation]</a> and bosonic symmetry that $$\label{asm:micro-mean-rms}
 \frac1{|\mathcal C_d|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal C_d}
       \|m_z-s_z\|_2^2
 \le\|(W_d)_1\Phi\|_2^2
 =\frac1D\frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(W_d)\rangle_\Phi
 \le D^{-1}(\epsilon_j+o(1))+o_K(1),$$ where $\epsilon_j\to0$ as $j\to\infty$, and the dilute remainder is taken at fixed $j$.

<a id="asm:R-rms-arithmetic"></a>
<a id="asm:R-mean-rms"></a>
For an $R$-cell $A$, the number $s_A(Y)$ is the root-mean-square of the numbers $s_z(Y)$ over its $d$-cells. Let $\overline s_A(Y)$ denote their arithmetic mean. Lemma <a href="#asm:rms-interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="asm:rms-interpolation">46</a> and <a href="#asm:all-local-pairs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:all-local-pairs">[asm:all-local-pairs]</a> imply $$\label{asm:R-rms-arithmetic}
 \frac1{|\mathcal C_R|}\sum_A\|s_A-\overline s_A\|_2^2
           =o(D^{-1})+o_K(1).$$ Also $m_A$ is the arithmetic mean of the $m_z$ in $A$. Jensen’s inequality, <a href="#asm:micro-mean-rms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:micro-mean-rms">[asm:micro-mean-rms]</a>, and <a href="#asm:R-rms-arithmetic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:R-rms-arithmetic">[asm:R-rms-arithmetic]</a> therefore give $$\label{asm:R-mean-rms}
 \frac1{|\mathcal C_R|}\sum_A\|m_A-s_A\|_2^2
       \le D^{-1}(2\epsilon_j+o(1))+o_K(1).$$

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
#### The infrared estimate and arbitrary ground vectors

<a id="stmt-asm-infrared"></a>
<div id="asm:infrared" class="proposition">

<a id="asm:infrared-bound"></a>
**Proposition 47** (Remaining excitations). *Put $Q_R=P_R-P_0$. Uniformly over normalized, possibly complex, bosonic ground vectors, $$\label{asm:infrared-bound}
 D\|(Q_R)_1\Phi\|_2^2\le16\epsilon_j+o(1)+o_K(1),$$ where $\epsilon_j\to0$. The coefficient $16$ is independent of $j$; the dilute remainder may depend on $j$. The exponents $u,w$ and the scale $R$ may be kept fixed when $j$ is increased; all dilute and volume thresholds may depend on $j$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-asm-infrared"><summary>Proof of stmt-asm-infrared</summary>

*Proof.* First let $\Phi\ge0$. For Hilbert-valued arrays, the variance is the minimum of the mean squared distance to a constant array. Comparing the array $m_A$ with the mean of the array $s_A$ thus yields $$\frac1{|\mathcal C_R|}\sum_A\|m_A-\overline m\|_2^2
 \le\frac2{|\mathcal C_R|}\sum_A\|m_A-s_A\|_2^2
      +\frac2{|\mathcal C_R|}\sum_A\|s_A-\overline s\|_2^2.$$ Here bars without cell subscripts denote averages over all $R$-cells. The left side is exactly $\|(P_R-P_0)_1\Phi\|_2^2$, by <a href="#asm:field-means" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:field-means">[asm:field-means]</a> and the normalized spatial measure. Equations <a href="#asm:rms-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:rms-variance">[asm:rms-variance]</a> and <a href="#asm:R-mean-rms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:R-mean-rms">[asm:R-mean-rms]</a> prove <a href="#asm:infrared-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:infrared-bound">[asm:infrared-bound]</a> for nonnegative vectors, with coefficient $4$ in place of $16$. The multiplication of a volume remainder by $D$ is harmless in the inner limit because $D\to D_*$.

For a real bosonic ground vector $f$, its positive and negative parts $f_+,f_-$ belong to the Dirichlet form domain and remain symmetric. The Sobolev truncation identities give $$\|f\|_2^2=\|f_+\|_2^2+\|f_-\|_2^2,
 \qquad q[f]=q[f_+]+q[f_-].$$ Each nonzero part has Rayleigh quotient at least $E_N$, and their sum attains $E_N$. Both nonzero parts therefore attain $E_N$ and are ground vectors. Complex conjugation commutes with the operator, so the real and imaginary parts of a complex ground vector are ground vectors or zero. A normalized $\Phi$ can consequently be written as $$\Phi=\sum_{a=1}^m c_a\Phi_a,
 \qquad m\le4,\quad \Phi_a\ge0,\quad\|\Phi_a\|_2=1,
 \qquad \sum_{a=1}^m|c_a|^2=1,$$ where each $\Phi_a$ is a ground vector. Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\|(Q_R)_1\Phi\|_2^2
       \le\sum_{a=1}^m\|(Q_R)_1\Phi_a\|_2^2.$$ Thus at most a factor four is lost in the vanishing error in <a href="#asm:infrared-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:infrared-bound">[asm:infrared-bound]</a>. No comparison of component phases or connectedness assumption is used. ◻

</details>

<a id="asm:depletion-decomposition"></a>
Because $P_0\le P_R$, $Q_R$ and $W_R$ are orthogonal projections with <a id="asm:orthogonal-decomposition"></a>$$\label{asm:orthogonal-decomposition}
 1-P_0=W_R+Q_R.$$ Consequently every normalized bosonic ground vector satisfies the exact identity $$\label{asm:depletion-decomposition}
 D(1-B(\Phi))=
        \frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(W_R)\rangle_\Phi
                   +D\|(Q_R)_1\Phi\|_2^2.$$ We record the conclusions in the per-volume normalization used for momentum observables: <a id="asm:coarse-density"></a><a id="asm:scaled-depletion"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(P_R-P_0)\rangle_\Phi
   &=D\|(P_R-P_0)_1\Phi\|_2^2=o(1),
       \label{asm:coarse-density}\\
 \frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(1-P_0)\rangle_\Phi
   &=D(1-B(\Phi))=\frac{8}{3\sqrt\pi}\alpha^{3/2}+o(1).
       \label{asm:scaled-depletion}
\end{aligned}$$ Both remainders are uniform over normalized, possibly complex ground vectors in the iterated limit of Section <a href="#set:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="set:section">2</a>. For the first, bosonic symmetry gives the equality, and Proposition <a href="#asm:infrared" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="asm:infrared">47</a> gives the error estimate: choose $j$ large and then increase the lower bound on $D$ and the volume threshold. The left side is independent of $j$, since $u,w,R$ remain fixed. The second follows from <a href="#asm:depletion-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:depletion-decomposition">[asm:depletion-decomposition]</a> and <a href="#en:R-occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:R-occupation">[en:R-occupation]</a>, whose energy estimate applies directly to complex ground vectors.

<a id="mom:section"></a>
### From local covariances to the momentum distribution

<span id="sec:momentum" label="sec:momentum"></span>

The local covariance estimate describes one-body operators inside a $B$-box, whereas momentum occupation is defined by Fourier modes on the whole torus. Translations connect these two descriptions: their expectations are the Fourier transform of the momentum measure. We first remove the remaining cell-constant excitations, then average the position of the $B$-box boundaries. This makes the cost of cutting a translation small compared with the excitation count.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### Translations and shifted box boundaries

<a id="mom:translation-identity"></a>
For a normalized ground vector $\Phi$ on $\Lambda_K$, put $u_{p,K}(y)=V^{-1/2}e^{ip\cdot y}$ and define the positive momentum measure <a id="mom:scaled-measure"></a>$$\label{mom:scaled-measure}
 \mathfrak m_\Phi
   =\frac1V\sum_{\substack{p\in(2\pi/K)\mathbb Z^3\\p\ne0}}
       \langle u_{p,K},\gamma^{(1)}_\Phi u_{p,K}\rangle\,\delta_p.$$ Here $\gamma^{(1)}_\Phi$ is the one-particle density matrix of $\Phi$ and $\delta_p$ is unit mass at $p$. Let $T_hg(y)=g(y+h)$ for $h\in\mathbb R^3$. Since $T_hu_{p,K}=e^{ih\cdot p}u_{p,K}$, $$\label{mom:translation-identity}
 \int e^{ih\cdot p}\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m_\Phi(p)
   =\frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma((1-P_0)T_h(1-P_0))\rangle_\Phi.$$

<a id="mom:excitation-masses"></a>
<a id="mom:remove-coarse"></a>
Recall $Q_R=P_R-P_0$ and $1-P_0=W_R+Q_R$. The inputs from <a href="#en:R-occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="en:R-occupation">[en:R-occupation]</a> and Proposition <a href="#asm:infrared" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="asm:infrared">47</a> give $$\label{mom:excitation-masses}
 a_\Phi:=\frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(W_R)\rangle_\Phi=O(1),
 \qquad
 b_\Phi:=\frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(Q_R)\rangle_\Phi=o(1).$$ For the second assertion, the operators and exponents can be held fixed as $j$ increases; one chooses $j$ first and the dilution threshold afterward. Both assertions are uniform over all complex ground vectors. Expanding <a href="#mom:translation-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mom:translation-identity">[mom:translation-identity]</a> and applying Cauchy–Schwarz to the sum over particles yields $$\label{mom:remove-coarse}
 \left|\int e^{ih\cdot p}\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m_\Phi(p)
       -\frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(W_RT_hW_R)\rangle_\Phi\right|
       \le b_\Phi+2\sqrt{a_\Phi b_\Phi}=o(1).$$ This bound is uniform in $h$. In particular, the subsequent boundary estimate will be multiplied by $a_\Phi$, the excitation density.

Fix $H<\infty$. Keep the $R$-grid fixed and let $\mathcal S$ be the family of $B$-box partitions obtained by shifting the original grid by $$\theta\in\{0,R,\ldots,B-R\}^3.$$ Every partition in this family is still a union of the same $R$-cells. For $\mathcal C\in\mathcal S$ define the translation cut at its box faces by <a id="mom:cut-translation"></a>$$\label{mom:cut-translation}
 T_h^{\mathcal C}=\sum_{b\in\mathcal C}\mathbf 1_bT_h\mathbf 1_b.$$ The average below is the uniform average over $\mathcal S$.

<a id="stmt-mom-shift-average"></a>
<div id="mom:shift-average" class="lemma">

<a id="mom:shift-norm"></a>
**Lemma 48** (Averaging box boundaries). *If $K>2(B+H)$ and $|h|\le H$, then $$\label{mom:shift-norm}
 \left\|T_h-\operatorname{Avg}_{\mathcal C\in\mathcal S}
                        T_h^{\mathcal C}\right\|
          \le C\frac{H+R}{B}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mom-shift-average"><summary>Proof of stmt-mom-shift-average</summary>

*Proof.* For fixed $y$, the difference is $T_h$ followed by multiplication by the fraction of partitions that put $y$ and $y+h$ in different boxes. In one coordinate, separation requires a box face between the two lifted coordinates. As the shift varies, possible faces are spaced by $R$, so at most $H/R+2$ of the $B/R$ shifts separate the pair. A union bound over the three coordinates proves the uniform multiplier bound <a href="#mom:shift-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mom:shift-norm">[mom:shift-norm]</a>. The count also applies across the periodic boundary; the stated restriction on $K$ allows the interval to be lifted without ambiguity. Cell faces themselves are null sets. ◻

</details>

<a id="mom:boundary-cost"></a>
Figure <a href="#fig:shifted-grid" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:shifted-grid">2</a> illustrates why the averaging is uniform in the position of the translated pair. Because $R/B\to0$ and $B\to\infty$, Lemma <a href="#mom:shift-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mom:shift-average">48</a> and <a href="#mom:excitation-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mom:excitation-masses">[mom:excitation-masses]</a> imply $$\label{mom:boundary-cost}
 \left|\frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(W_RT_hW_R)\rangle_\Phi
  -\operatorname{Avg}_{\mathcal C\in\mathcal S}
      \frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(W_RT_h^{\mathcal C}W_R)\rangle_\Phi\right|
       \le C\frac{H+R}{B}\,a_\Phi=o(1).$$ The number of shifted grids creates no additional factor in this estimate. The replacement of the full particle density $D$ by $a_\Phi$ is essential, since $D(H+R)/B$ does not tend to zero at the chosen scales.

<figure id="fig:shifted-grid">

<figcaption>A one-dimensional section of the shifted partitions, with $B = 4R$ and a fixed pair $y, y + h$. Thin ticks mark the same $R$-grid in every row; thick ticks are the $B$-box faces. In the first row the translation crosses a box face and is cut. In the other three rows it stays inside one box. In general, only $O(H/R+1)$ of the $B/R$ shifts can separate a pair with $|h| ≤ H$. Sandwiching the averaged error by $W_{R}$ makes its expectation cost proportional to the excitation density, as in <a href="#mom:boundary-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mom:boundary-cost">[mom:boundary-cost]</a>.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-shifted-grid"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:shifted-grid`)

   \draw[gray!65] (0,0)--(8,0);

    \foreach \x in {0,4,8} {\draw[very thick] (\x,-.29)--(\x,.29);}

    \fill[BrickRed] (4.45,.15) circle (1.5pt);

   \else
    \draw[very thick] (\shift,-.29)--(\shift,.29);
    \draw[very thick] (\shift+4,-.29)--(\shift+4,.29);

    \fill[MidnightBlue] (4.45,.15) circle (1.5pt);

   \fi

 \node[anchor=south] at (3.65,.43) {$y$};

  - A double-headed horizontal arrow indicating length $B = 4R$ spanning 4 grid units.
  - A double-headed horizontal arrow indicating length $R$ spanning 1 grid unit.
  - A pair of positions $y$ and $y + h$ marked by vertical bars, connected by a blue horizontal vector with an arrow pointing from $y$ to $y + h$.
- Four horizontal axis lines corresponding to different shift parameters:
  - Row 1 labeled "$\theta = 0$": has thin vertical tick marks spaced by $R$, and a thick vertical tick mark marking a $B$-box face that falls directly between $y$ and $y + h$; labeled on the right as "cut".
  - Row 2 labeled "$\theta = R$": thin ticks remain aligned with the $R$-grid, while the thick tick marks are shifted by distance $R$ to the right; the segment from $y$ to $y + h$ does not cross a thick tick; labeled on the right as "retained".
  - Row 3 labeled "$\theta = 2R$": thick tick marks are shifted by $2R$ to the right; labeled on the right as "retained".
  - Row 4 labeled "$\theta = 3R$": thick tick marks are shifted by $3R$ to the right; labeled on the right as "retained".
